- [2026-08-28] **#1642 was already fixed by #1690/#1695 (`5610638a7`), filed independently against the same first-ever occurrence of the flake before that fix landed.** #1642 reports `src/editor/EditorShell.test.tsx`'s "retires a read taken during the episode when Recreate publishes the bytes itself" failing `expected 266 to be 4` on run `31971920058` (base `d67b85d5f837dd34e8997c189e79180834aa0658`), attributing it to host contention rather than a logic inversion. That base commit is a real ancestor of `5610638a7` (`git merge-base --is-ancestor` confirms it, and `5610638a7` is already on current `main`), and `5610638a7`'s own PR body names the identical test and an identical mechanism: the fixture's `openDocumentMock` had no safe default (a bare `vi.fn()` resolving `undefined` once FR-ED-7's self-rearming poll outran every queued answer, #368's fixture-default class recurring one layer down), and this specific case additionally captured its expected read total too early and asserted it *after* releasing the held read — which rearms the poll onto an already-settled `mockReturnValue`, so it issues a fresh real read every polled millisecond straight into the assertion window. `#1690`'s own body (closed, fixed by `#1695`) cites this exact test failing on a later run (`32057778461`, head `af60304d2`, PR #1682, `2026-08-17T18:58:46Z`) than #1642's own (`31971920058`, `2026-08-16T20:54:38Z` — #1642 is first by ~22 hours), confirming #1642 and #1690 are the same defect, filed by two different discovery paths before either was linked to the other. The fix — recorded in this journal's `#1690` entry below (line 526) with controlled-load A/B evidence (pre-fix 6/6 red under a derived 25 ms stall, post-fix 0/6) — asserts the read-count immediately after the Missing exit rather than after settling the held read, and parks a second held read before releasing the first, so no assertion in the case has a live poll left to race. No product code changed in `5610638a7`; `EditorShell.tsx`'s FR-ED-7 poller is untouched by both that fix and this finding. Verified here: `origin/main` at `ce6173c4c` (this card's rebased base) already contains `5610638a7`; the named test alone passed 50/50 direct `vitest run -t` invocations, 20 of them run concurrently against two independent full-suite (`npx vitest run`, 99 files/1533 tests each) processes to reproduce the contention class #1642 hypothesizes, and the full `npm test` suite passed 99 files/1533 tests clean — a lighter ambient load than the #1690 entry below records as insufficient on its own (six repeats under 24 CPU hogs at load average 32 stayed green there too, and "0-of-N at a single-digit rate is weak evidence"), and this ran against post-fix code where the settle-rearm window is structurally gone, so it could not have gone red either way; the disposition rests on the ancestry proof and #1695's own amplified 6/6→0/6 A/B, not on this run. Closing #1642 with no code/test/matrix change (`docs/test-matrix.md`'s `FR-ED-7` row already carries `#1690`'s fixture-default fix); reopen only if the exact `expected N to be M` count assertion reproduces against current `main`.
- [2026-08-27] **#1995: Claude Code 2.1.247 separates its secure-storage selector from disposable `HOME`, but only an explicitly empty selector reaches the default macOS Keychain item.** The retained live qualification process had the correct five-name cell environment and therefore could not authenticate: with disposable `HOME`, Claude derived a different secure-storage identity and returned `authentication_failed` with zero tokens/cost. Source recovered from the installed standalone binary, without executing it, shows that `CLAUDE_SECURESTORAGE_CONFIG_DIR` has three distinct states: unset follows the configuration/home-derived identity; non-empty hashes that exact value into a different Keychain service; empty selects the unsuffixed default `Claude Code-credentials` service while file fallback remains under disposable `HOME/.claude`. The experiment route now constructs that empty value itself after authenticating the immutable five-name cell environment; it never inherits an ambient value or copies a token/path. This preserves TD-23's division: host authentication remains inside the trusted provider process, while settings, customization, credential bytes, host `HOME`, and control-plane locators stay absent. The binding is carried in the exact launch-policy digest and #1995 now derives its credential-environment observation from the real composed launch environment rather than the provider-neutral five-name manifest alone. Another executable/version/OS tuple still requires its own qualification.
- [2026-08-26] **#1813: a handled read rejection is not a successful empty answer.** Timeline transcript rehydration previously collapsed resolve-empty and reject into one `transcriptSettled` bit, so a disk/bridge failure rendered `No activity yet` — an affirmative history claim the read never established. The ratified state keeps the three facts separate (`loading` / `loaded` / `failed`): only `loaded` may unlock facet-empty copy; `failed` preserves every in-memory card and adds the established compact unavailable treatment with a retry that increments only the transcript-load attempt. The useful test boundary is coexistence, not merely catch coverage: failure UI and a retained card must be visible at the same time, while the retry proof requires the IPC trace to contain exactly two `load_transcript` calls and no collateral command.
- [2026-08-26] **#1420: a provider's delivery announcement is an intention; persisted work is the outcome that may replace its prose.** The #1118 reply channel correctly suppresses a terminal explanation beside a tracked-change or comment card, but its first implementation used `AgentSession::saw_delivery` — a fold over streamed `tool_use` names — as evidence that such a card existed. A wholly-stale `propose_changes`, refused MCP call, or permission denial can carry that same announcement and then persist nothing; suppressing its result leaves only a completed working card and no explanation. The correction preserves CJ's #1118 ruling rather than reinterpreting it: the open-project aggregate now shares one run-scoped outcome tracker between its bound MCP server and queue observer, and the three delivery tools mark it only after the document/change-set or comment event is durably written. `RunPhase::Completed` consults that fact, so an empty delivery keeps the provider's explanation and a real delivery still suppresses it. `saw_delivery` remains intentionally unchanged for J7 stopped-run honesty (#1404), where a kill can race the tool boundary and the stream announcement is the existing conservative approximation. The reusable boundary is: use announcements for activity/interrupt approximation; use committed side effects for terminal outcome claims.
- [2026-08-24] **CJ approved #1934's real-Git proof boundary for #1937–#1939: consumer state-machine tests may replace repeated local-remote transport with real bare-object persistence and atomic old-OID CAS, while one focused two-clone contract retains the transport proof.** #1937 implements the boundary without changing production code: `bareGitRefs` writes real blobs, trees, commits, and refs in a shared bare object database; fresh clients read the persisted bytes; and `git update-ref <ref> <new> <old>` elects one create/update winner and rejects stale mutation. Exactly one direct `realGitRefs` contract keeps the distinct proof that clone-only commit objects transfer to `origin`, both create and update pushes carry the intended `--force-with-lease=<ref>:<expected>` form, competing clones converge on one remote winner, and authority traffic leaves both checkouts clean and unmoved. The five serialized consumers remain owned by #1938, the lane/conflict-domain consumers and private-adapter removal remain owned by #1939, no behavioral assertion is deleted here, and #1188's inexpensive genuine-commit-identity control remains unchanged. This is the reviewed-proof substitution CJ explicitly approved on [issue #1937](../../../issues/1937#issuecomment-5403676302), not a D#/TD#/FR-*/J#/P#/DH# delta.

- [2026-08-23] **Advancing an injectable clock is deterministic about virtual time, not about how much real work every tick performs (#1699).** `does not carry a Keep-mine dismissal across the loss of the path's identity` advanced 5,000 ms to make its 1,000 ms autosave due while an injected 1 ms external-change poll was also armed. Its `editWhileMissing` barrier was installed as the permanent `openDocument` answer; once settled, all later polls received that already-settled promise. The virtual-time advance therefore drove 5,000 promise round trips plus React work before reaching its assertions, and machine contention could push that deterministic workload past Vitest's 5 s wall-time budget. The amplified guard measured **5,006 reads where at most one post-settle poll is admissible**. The repair leaves the barrier shared while the ordering needs multiple concurrent callers, then restores the fixture's existing never-settled `pendingDocumentRead` only after this cell's reappearance read is in flight and before settling it. The same 5,000 ms advance now proves the autosave boundary without spinning the poll. No timeout, `waitFor`, or production behavior changed; the focused cell fell from 235 ms pre-fix to 156 ms and all 118 `EditorShell` cells pass. The first attempt narrowed the shared helper to one caller and broke three sibling orderings that deliberately share a read; scoping the park to the one test preserves those distinct seams.

- [2026-08-23] **#1780: a passive transition initializer can overwrite an action taken against the committed state it initializes.** `EditorShell` already kept `missingBannerOpen` true while a document was not Missing, so the first Missing render synchronously carried J2's recovery banner. Its passive Missing-entry effect nevertheless set that state true again. An author who clicked `[Keep editing]` after the Missing DOM committed but before the passive effect ran queued `false` first; the effect then queued `true` and reopened the banner. Full-suite and Windows load widened that real commit-to-effect window, which is why the existing synchronous dismissal assertion usually passed and intermittently received the original banner. The deterministic reproduction belongs in `EditorShell.scheduling.test.tsx`, outside Testing Library's `act` environment: a layout-effect sibling clicks the real committed button, and a passive-effect DOM witness proves the later effect updates have committed before the banner absence is asserted. Re-adding the one redundant `setMissingBannerOpen(true)` makes that cell fail with the issue's exact stale banner; removing it leaves initial and later Missing entries unchanged because the non-Missing branch still primes the next entry, while the author's dismissal can no longer be overwritten. The focus variant recorded on the issue was a separate sibling scheduling mistake already fixed by #1700/#1850: it awaited a parent commit before synchronously asserting a child passive focus effect.

- [2026-08-21] **#1700: waiting for one commit's DOM result does not wait for a sibling passive effect, even when both updates came from one user action.** The `TabbedEditor` dirty-close cell waited until the parent removed its `alertdialog`, then synchronously asserted that `EditorShell` had focused Keep mine. Those are deliberately different phases: the blocked imperative save first calls `TabbedEditor.onExternalChangeSaveBlocked`, whose state update removes the guard in the parent commit, then increments `externalChangeAttentionRequest`; `EditorShell` responds from `useEffect`, after that commit, because the background FR-ED-7 banner must never steal focus on mount and only the user's explicit Save may request it. Under load, the assertion could run in the real commit→passive-effect window and saw `<body>` — exactly run `32063315939`'s failure — even though the focus request was correct and merely pending. This was measured, not inferred: temporarily delaying only the existing effect's `focus()` by 25 ms made the unmodified cell fail deterministically at `TabbedEditor.test.tsx:476` with the reported expected Keep mine / received `<body>` signature; changing only that assertion to a condition-based `waitFor` made the same delayed arm pass, and removing the delay left the focused cell green. No production code changed. The general rule is narrower than “wrap focus assertions in `waitFor`”: await the condition owned by the asynchronous phase being asserted. A wait on an earlier DOM consequence proves that consequence and nothing about later passive effects, even when React batches their state requests from the same promise continuation.

- [2026-08-20] **A fix for an orphaned seam can relocate the seam instead of closing it — and the tests that prove the fix are exactly the ones blind to the relocation (#1723, PR #1791 review round 2, Important).** #1723 existed because `commands::agent::spawn_claude_run` hardcoded `resume_session_id: None`, a caller-side blind spot no `build_args`-only test could see. The fix moved that read one level up into `commands::agent::bound_server` — and put the *same* blind spot there: mutating `bound_server` to `Ok((project.mcp_server(), None))` left `cargo test --workspace` at **1167 passed, 0 failed** — D4 continuity silently disabled, not one cell reddened. (That whole-workspace figure is the reviewer's measurement, reported in PR #1791's round-2 report; what I re-ran myself under the same mutation was the module's own scope, `21 passed; 1 failed`, with the newly added cell the only red.) **Why the three new pins all missed it is the transferable part: each one stopped at, or restarted from, the seam under test rather than crossing it.** `claude_invocation_appends_resume_when_a_session_id_is_given` hands `Some(id)` in by hand, so it never asks where a real caller gets one. `a_freshly_opened_projects_bound_server_carries_no_resume_session_id` is the only cell that calls `bound_server` about the field, and it asserts `None` — which the mutant also returns, so a negative-only pin on a seam whose defect *is* `None` is structurally vacuous. And the integration test read `project.resume_session_id()` directly, its own helper doc admitting it reads it "the same way `commands::agent::bound_server` reads it in production" — i.e. it **reimplemented** the production read instead of exercising it, which is the same shape as a fake that echoes back what the fixture just wrote. **The rule I'd write from this: when a card's defect is "a caller hardcoded a value", the fix is not done until a test crosses the *new* caller boundary in the positive direction.** A private function is not an excuse to hand-wire around it — `bound_server` is private, which is precisely why the integration test reimplemented the read, and precisely why the closing cell had to be a unit test inside `commands::agent`'s own `mod tests`. Two cheap checks would have caught it before review: ask of every new production edge "what mutation of *this line* reddens something?" (the PR's own mutation table declared four rows and none of them touched `bound_server`'s read), and treat a `None`-asserting test on a nullable seam as covering nothing until a `Some`-asserting sibling brackets it. The declared-mutation habit is only as good as its coverage of edges — a table of four reproduced rows still shipped a fifth edge with no row at all.

- [2026-08-19] **A measurement whose zero point is its own first observation reports zero for the case it was built to measure.** #1762 review round 1, both Importants, one root cause. The watchdog's blind-spot accumulator opened an episode *at* the first failed sample (`unreadableSinceMs ?? nowMs`), so `currentUnreadableMs = max(0, nowMs - nowMs) = 0`. For a **sustained** outage that is harmless — the streak grows on every subsequent sample and the gating threshold works — which is exactly why it survived my own tests, all of which drove multi-sample outages. For an episode of **one** sample it is catastrophic to the artifact: the two duration fields the runbook tells an operator to read reported a blind spot of **zero milliseconds** for the single transient blip the whole card exists to tolerate. The fix is one line (open the episode at the attempt's *start* clock, not its end) but the lesson is the test shape: **I never drove the modal case.** My outages were 20 minutes because that was the interesting axis for the budget; the blip that motivated the card was one reset connection lasting seconds, and no cell existed for it. When a change is justified by a *frequent small* event and gated on a *rare large* one, the small one needs its own cell — it is not a weaker version of the large one, it is a different code path through the same accumulator. The second Important fell straight out of the first: because a one-sample episode opens and closes immediately, every isolated blip costs **two** degraded-flag transitions, so my "a six-day window costs a handful of durable writes" became 344 writes over 400 samples against a flapping authority. A per-transition bound stated as a per-window number is false the moment transitions stop being rare — and I had written that number into four places (module header, runbook, test-matrix, PR body) on the strength of one measurement of one steady scenario. **Measure a cost claim under the adversarial input before writing it into a permanent record, or state the bound in the unit it is actually bounded in.** Also worth keeping: `Object.freeze(new Set([...]))` freezes nothing — a Set's entries live in internal slots, so `.add()` still succeeds. I had used it to make a trigger list "structural"; a frozen array with `.includes` is the thing that actually holds.

- [2026-08-17] **`GET .../actions/runs/{run_id}/jobs` returns only the LATEST attempt's jobs, and #1403's overlap measurement was wrong until independent review caught it.** The endpoint silently omits every earlier attempt's jobs on a re-run — the response's only top-level keys are `jobs` and `total_count` (there is no truncation flag), and `total_count` reports only the returned attempt's job count (3 for run `31958141186`) rather than the true cross-attempt total (6). Two signals survive the drop: `run_attempt` appears on every job object and on the run object itself, and the run object's `previous_attempt_url` is set to the prior attempt's URL when `run_attempt > 1` and `null` otherwise (verified: re-run runs `31958141186`/`31984294760` carry it, single-attempt runs `32037610888`/`31905694715` are `null`); `run.run_attempt > 1` is the operationally load-bearing check, since enumerating `1..run_attempt` needs the count and `previous_attempt_url` only a one-step back-link, but either tells a caller earlier attempts exist even though this call will not list their jobs. `GET .../actions/runs/{run_id}/attempts/{attempt_number}/jobs`, enumerated over every `run_attempt`, is the honest source. #1403's promotion required — per its 2026-08-16 comment — at least 5 of the first 20 qualifying Windows E2E executions to overlap another Windows job; measured through the run-scoped endpoint the count read 18 of 20. Two of the twenty belonged to re-run runs, and for those the concurrent attempt-1 sibling `Suites` job was invisible via the run-scoped endpoint — while the attempt-2 burn-in job that *was* visible is a clone carrying attempt-1's timestamps — so the comparison landed between a real burn-in interval and a `Suites` sibling from the wrong attempt and concluded "no overlap." The true count is 20 of 20. Concretely, run `31958141186`: burn-in `95191880943` ran 16:16:49–16:19:20Z on `cowrite-windows-2`; its real concurrent sibling, attempt-1's `95191880889`, ran 16:16:49–16:23:48Z on `cowrite-windows` and does not appear via the run-scoped endpoint; what got measured instead was attempt-2's `95193098842`, 16:26:59–16:35:31Z — ten minutes later, no overlap, wrong conclusion. Run `31984294760` has the identical shape. Caught at independent review, not by the original measurement. **The generalizable lesson, worth recording separately from a one-off endpoint quirk: a count queried through an API's default view cannot detect a finding about that default.** This is the same shape as the 2026-08-13 `#1456` entry above — `ProjectV2.items` silently excludes archived items by default, and an exact `node(id:)` read surfaces `isArchived` (the #1456 fix used that route rather than adding `archivedStates` filtering as an alternative); here the run-scoped jobs endpoint silently excludes prior attempts, and only the attempt-scoped endpoint surfaces them. Neither omission is visible from inside the narrower call, which is exactly what makes it dangerous to build a measurement on. **A related trap, same endpoint family:** `runs/{run_id}/jobs` returned an HTTP 404 for two runs on this repo — `32037610888` and `31905694715` — observed on 2026-08-17, while the attempt-scoped endpoint served them normally at the time. It did not reproduce afterward: both runs, plus the 30 most recent workflow runs on this repo, returned HTTP 200 on every subsequent call (32/32). Treat it as a transient fault observed on those two runs, not as a property of any particular run or as something reliably reproducible from these run ids. Distinguish a transient failure from a definite answer: retrying it into an exception takes a probe blind, and silently treating it as "no jobs" under-counts the same way the attempt-scoping does. Note the direction of the error for the overlap criterion: it is conservative, under-counting overlap rather than inflating it, and #1403's promotion criteria were satisfied either way (18/20 and 20/20 both clear the 5/20 bar). The same default could equally have hidden an earlier attempt's terminal failure — eligibility-inflating rather than conservative; that did not happen here only because both re-run attempts' visible burn-in jobs are clones carrying the original attempt's successful conclusion and timestamps. That the risk was closed rather than merely reasoned about: PR #1596's review at head `389da22` (comment 5317405909) independently enumerated every attempt of every `windows-self-hosted.yml` run in its one-day observation window and found exactly 20 PR-triggered burn-in executions with a successful `Playwright acceptance suite` step and zero with a failed one.

- [2026-08-15] **#1315 round 3: the tick-flush loop's own claim that it "cannot outrun vitest's 5000ms default" was false, disproved by execution — a tick-count bound is immune to contention on the number of ticks needed, not on the wall-clock cost of paying for them.** Round 2 (previous entry) shipped `maxTicks = 200` on the strength of that claim. Five exhaustion runs at load ≈20–22 (reviewer's own measurement) already showed 1/5 losing the real diagnostic to a bare `Error: Test timed out in 5000ms.`, typical exhaustion 3.2–3.5s against the 5000ms ceiling. Re-measured directly against #1315's own target load (this session generated 210–228 via a background `cargo test --workspace` plus up to 120 `yes` burners — at and above the card's originally reported 179–201): at `maxTicks = 200` exhaustion was not re-measured at this load (too slow to be worth running), but at `maxTicks = 20` it failed to preserve the diagnostic 5/5, each run hitting the bare vitest timeout instead of `TestingLibraryElementError`. More strikingly, a **legitimately passing** run of the same test — no exhaustion, no injected failure — hit the same bare vitest timeout **1 run in 10** at this load, purely from the fixed cost of mounting `App` and its module mocks under contention. **The tick bound cannot fix either failure mode**: it governs the loop's own iteration count, not vitest's per-test ceiling sitting outside it, and that ceiling can fire on the base render alone before the loop is ever reached. **The corrected sizing.** `maxTicks` was reduced to `10` — a 10x margin over the measured maximum real need of 1 tick (never more, across every test in the block), chosen on evidence rather than comfort: at the same heavy load, `maxTicks = 20`'s own exhaustion (8.9–11.2s, 5/5 runs, verified with a temporarily raised ceiling) took measurably longer than `maxTicks = 10`'s (5.8–7.4s, 5/5 runs) for identical headroom purposes — each additional tick costs more, not less, the more contended the machine is. A per-`it()` vitest timeout of `20000` ms was reintroduced, sized against the worst exhaustion actually measured at `maxTicks = 10` under that load (7.4s), giving roughly 2.7x headroom; re-verified against the exact shipped code (not a CLI override) with an injected always-failing role query: 3/3 runs at 367–370ms under lighter ambient load (~30–137) still correctly threw the real `TestingLibraryElementError`. This is not round 1's flaw recurring: round 1's `{ timeout: 8000 }` on `waitFor` *was* the success condition's own ceiling, sized against a probe that could not falsify it because probe and ceiling were co-designed. Here the success condition is the tick count alone, unaffected by this number — a passing test that only ever needs 1 tick finishes in ~3–4s whether this timeout reads 5000 or 20000; the number exists solely so vitest's own unrelated ceiling does not preempt an already-finishing (tick-bounded) loop, or the base render itself, before either can report what it found. **General rule: "bounded by iteration count, not wall-clock time" describes what governs *whether* a wait succeeds, not what it costs to find out — every tick still spends real time, and under severe contention that per-tick cost, and even fixed one-time setup cost, can themselves approach an unrelated ceiling nobody sized against this failure mode. Test the failure path at the target load, not just the success path, and size any bound — tick count or wall-clock — against a number you actually measured at that load, never a number that "should" be safe.**

- [2026-08-15] **#1315: `App.test.tsx`'s comment-thread-ownership `waitFor`s were budget-based, not condition-based — direct CPU-contention reproduction failed on this host, so the losing order was constructed deterministically instead, and review round 2 corrected the fix from a larger wall-clock budget to a tick-count bound.** The card's own evidence (concurrent `cargo test --workspace`, whole file) could not be reproduced by matching that setup here: a background `cargo test --workspace` (cold `target/`, `sccache`-warmed dep graph) plus up to 76 `yes` burners drove one-minute load average to 179–201 without ever failing the named test across ~10 full-suite and filtered runs — the machine has 12 cores and a fast build, and ambient load from other concurrently-running engineer sessions on the same box (independently observed at load average 30–200) made attribution unreliable besides. Per the #933/#1448 precedent ("the async boundary is usually a mocked promise you already control, and forcing it is cheaper than another twenty runs"), the losing order was constructed directly instead: `loadCommentThreadsMock.mockImplementationOnce` returning a promise that resolves after a real 6000ms `setTimeout` reproduced the exact reported failure — `TestingLibraryElementError: Unable to find role="button" and name \`/Address all/\`` at the same `waitFor` — deterministically, at the pre-fix head, on an otherwise idle machine. That construction **proves the mechanism** — that RTL's default 1000ms polling ceiling is what actually fires — and no more than that; it does not by itself validate any particular replacement ceiling. **The corrected fix (review round 2).** The first fix raised `waitFor`'s timeout to 8000ms and every `it()`'s own vitest timeout to 15000ms, chosen as "the probe's 6000ms delay plus margin" — a number the probe that produced it could not have falsified, since the two were co-designed. Review round 2 identified that every hop in the chain this describe block waits on (`openProject` → `request` → `activeDocumentPath` → `loadCommentThreads`, a mocked promise with no timer → `commentThreads` → commit) is entirely microtasks: **CPU contention changes how long a tick takes, never how many ticks the cascade needs**, so the condition-based fix is a bounded tick-flush loop (retry the condition, `await act(async () => {})` to drain the microtask queue, repeat up to a fixed iteration count) rather than a larger wall-clock ceiling, which only tolerates contention instead of being immune to it. The shipped `waitForAsyncRender` helper is exactly that loop (`maxTicks = 200`, later corrected to `10` — see the following entry, round 3), with the same `(condition) => Promise<T>` signature `waitFor` already had, so every call site changed only in name. Non-vacuity was verified directly, not assumed: a deliberately-unmatchable role name still exhausts the bound and rethrows the real `TestingLibraryElementError`, so a genuine regression reports the same diagnostic it always did (measured exhaustion cost: 3.47s for the single failing test, consistent with the reviewer's own 3.14s measurement of the same shape). Scoped to this file/describe block only, per the card's own authorization — not a `vitest.config.ts` global change (docs/journal.md 2026-07-20 #368 item 4 reserves that for CJ's sign-off) — and confined to the reported `#411` block; ~168 other plain `waitFor(` calls at the 1000ms default remain untouched elsewhere in `src/App.test.tsx` (YAGNI). **General rule: constructing the losing order (a real, controlled delay on the mocked async boundary) is the right way to prove *that* a wall-clock ceiling is the failure mechanism — it is not, by itself, evidence for *what number* should replace it, because a probe co-designed with its own ceiling cannot falsify that ceiling. Before reaching for a bigger number, ask whether the wait is polling a purely-microtask cascade — if it is, a tick-count bound is immune to contention by construction, where any wall-clock number is merely more tolerant of it.**

- [2026-08-14] **#1262: the parked measurement answered by collapsing, and a rationale priced in coverage cannot survive a population of zero.** The isolation canary's `concurrency` group was parked on 2026-08-07 pending a post-#1234 measurement of canary-vs-canary contention, deliberately expressed as runs-per-merged-PR rather than a last-N cancellation rate. #1266 then made `workflow_dispatch:` the canary's only trigger (`3678d98c4`, 2026-08-08T07:07:16Z), and the re-measurement on 2026-08-14 came back empty: **0 canary runs across the 119 PRs merged since**, runs-per-merged-PR 0.00, 0 evictions, with the last canary run of any kind (`31242969798`) predating that merge by an hour. **Decision (CJ, 2026-08-14): the global group `windows-runner-isolation-canary` stands.** Per-ref keying's entire case was *"every PR keeps its own canary"*; no PR produces one now, so it buys nothing, while its cost survives dispatch-only and bites harder — two dispatches on different refs would become co-eligible for the same two-runner pool, which is the #1231 starvation the group exists to prevent. The other option, an external re-dispatcher so a displaced run queues instead of vanishing, is real machinery bought against a measured zero, and #1261's `workflow_run` observer already makes an eviction legible from outside. What is **not** decided is canary-vs-*production* contention, which stays #1266's and its follow-ons'. The outcome is recorded in three places that must move together — the workflow's `concurrency:` comment, `docs/ci-windows-runner.md`, and a guard that pins that record *and* requires it to name the group value it froze, so a silent re-key cannot leave behind a decision note describing a keying nobody uses. This is #1347's sibling, and the vocabulary finally lands where it belongs: "the global group stands" is true here, on a workflow that actually has one, and was wrong when imported into #1347's entry about a workflow #982 had already left ungrouped. **General rule: when a change removes the trigger a parked measurement was going to sample, do not re-run the measurement and report "still no data" — check first whether that removal already answered the question by deleting the population the deferred option existed to serve.**

- [2026-08-14] **#1536: before increasing an asynchronous browser assertion's tolerance, verify that its target represents the behavior the test claims.** A retries-disabled burn-in of D23's representative-prose acceptance case reproduced the `Mod+End` containment failure by iteration 3 and repeatedly thereafter. Browser geometry and selection tracing showed that navigation had reached the true document end: the source ends in `\n`, CodeMirror rendered the corresponding post-newline position as a final empty `.cm-line`, and the DOM selection sat there. The outer editor host was scrolled to within one line-height of its maximum, leaving most of only that empty boundary line below the host; the actual last content-bearing line (`---`) was fully contained. The previous #1408 polling change stabilized when the geometry was sampled but left this semantic mismatch intact. The acceptance helper now checks containment of the last non-empty line, while the same test's exact `END_809::` insertion and saved-byte assertion continue to prove that the caret itself reached the true end. The identical one-worker, no-retry burn-in passed 100/100 after that one-line locator change. **General rule:** use an independent observable to prove navigation and aim visual containment at visible content; longer waits cannot make an empty boundary line into the last content-bearing line.
- [2026-08-14] **#1518: TD-18's unbounded scan now has a reproducible real-scanner cost envelope, but two warm local-volume hosts do not establish a defensible universal acceptability threshold.** `cargo bench --bench project_files_scan` generates separate visible trees of exactly 1,000, 10,000, and 50,000 files (5, 44, and 220 visible directories), warms each once, then times five complete scans through the production `FilesystemScanner::scan`; fixture creation/deletion is outside the timed region and every iteration asserts the exact file/directory counts and zero warnings. On an Apple M4 Pro (12 logical CPUs), arm64 macOS 26.5.2 build 25F84, APFS local volume, the median/p95 were **23.73/24.36 ms**, **333.81/338.92 ms**, and **1,536.44/1,552.29 ms**. On exact branch revision `102766cf196548d8ac907e1e71a550d93e895a0c` in manual self-hosted Windows run `31824925010`, an AMD Ryzen 7 7840HS (16 logical CPUs), Windows X64 local workspace measured **38.04/39.07 ms**, **600.01/669.10 ms**, and **2,432.94/2,442.54 ms**. The benchmark step, Rust checks, tooling/matrix checks, and E2E job all passed; the encompassing manual suite job later failed the already-observed transient `EditorShell.test.tsx` reload assertion (1/1,282), outside this Rust/workflow delta. That transient did not recur in fully green exact-branch run `31827031129` or the following final-head E2E job. Exact live-main revision `cd6557da9dc9b5fd6cf3978c2a26c0396e27a62a` then confirmed a fixed NTFS workspace at **36.17/37.09 ms**, **481.53/487.06 ms**, and **2,407.75/2,417.47 ms**; adding the benchmark made the aggregate exceed #835's fixed 15-minute job budget, canceling during matrix validation after every preceding step passed. The first correction reserved 20 minutes only for manual dispatch, but exact-head required PR run `31858981497` then exhausted the retained 15-minute branch during Vitest despite correctly skipping the benchmark; exact-head manual run `31857837131` completed the full aggregate, benchmark included, in 16m05. Every Windows Suites event therefore receives the same bounded 20 minutes while the benchmark remains operator-only. The measurements are roughly linear and show no discontinuity: 1k is tens of milliseconds, 10k remains subsecond, and 50k is a real multi-second cost. That last point is an evidence trigger for future reassessment using observed project workloads and network-volume measurements, not evidence for a number at which Cowrite should silently stop: two local-volume machines do not cover slower disks, network mounts, user tolerance, or the warning UX a ceiling would require. Therefore this change adds **no TD-18 entry bound, skip rule, or optimization** and preserves the current unbounded scan contract. Revisit only with representative workload/volume evidence; any ceiling remains a recorded-decision delta requiring CJ's ratification rather than an inference from these two hosts.

- [2026-08-14] **#1083: a resource cap that lowers the saturation percentile still does not qualify when the preserved completion bound is the actual bottleneck.** The operator-only Windows benchmark now takes one explicit `cargo_build_jobs` value (`uncapped` or a canonical positive integer), stamps it into every run/slot record, and preserves CPU/disk/queue summaries in durable logs as well as Actions summaries. The uncapped path must *remove* `CARGO_BUILD_JOBS` at each Cargo step: exporting an empty string is not Cargo's default but an immediate parse error, as invalid run `31804343842` proved. The reserved immutable-target experiment then established a valid single-worker control (`S = 667s`, three complete runs) but could not establish either dual arm. Uncapped dual run `31814177457` held both CPUs at P90 100% and timed out both jobs in matrix validation before vitest. Jobs=8 dual run `31815640383` reduced CPU P90 to 96% and disk maxima from 89/115% to 32/32%, yet both jobs still hit the unchanged 15-minute bound—one in matrix validation and one in vitest. That is a useful host-pressure change and still a failed acceptance result: incomplete production suites cannot enter `D` or `M`, so throughput `2S / M` is not calculable and the cap cannot reach production. The experiment stopped at its authorized bound, preserved the unspent uncapped retry, and left `.github/workflows/windows-self-hosted.yml` untouched. A separate operational lesson came from invalid run `31805058089`: a quiet pool observed before dispatch is not a reservation; unrelated Windows/E2E jobs entered later and correctly invalidated the complete measured window. **General rule:** unset an optional tool knob instead of encoding “default” as an empty value, reserve the whole measurement window rather than only its first instant, and never promote a favorable subsystem metric when the end-to-end validity gate did not complete.

- [2026-08-14] **#1449's Markdown-toggle scroll-equality observation remains unresolved after a bounded non-reproduction; the evidence does not assign it to #368 or to a layout/order defect.** The card came from one full Playwright run on PR #1437's `4daa45e625b8f4fb642eeb18869fd3568f86da44` head: 33 passed / 1 failed at the exact-equality poll after the Markdown toggle, followed by 3/3 isolated passes while the host load average was 5.7–6.9. The Engineer's pinned `origin/main` head at dispatch, `9e0982a56c0c7acfac1c7c5d16a27a546168c9cb`, passed the unchanged full acceptance suite 33/33 (55.1s; target 1.2s), and `git diff --exit-code 4daa45e625b8f4fb642eeb18869fd3568f86da44..9e0982a56c0c7acfac1c7c5d16a27a546168c9cb -- e2e/acceptance.spec.mjs src/editor/ViewModeToolbar.tsx src/editor/viewMode.ts src/editor/structuralDecorations.ts src/editor/trackedChanges.ts` plus the matching path-limited `git log` were empty. That proves no change in those paths explains the later green run; it does **not** prove the original observation's cause. The first stress attempt used 24 CPU burners and made the target fail twice only at unrelated `ENOTEMPTY` fixture cleanup, while its scroll assertion stayed green 20/20 in the one-worker run; the independent review then reproduced neither failure across another 32 stressed target runs. Those teardown failures therefore carry no causal weight for the scroll observation. A closer match to the original topology ran the full 33-test suite with one Playwright worker while `cargo test --workspace` ran concurrently, starting at load average 6.69: Playwright passed 33/33 in 39.9s (target 1.5s) and Cargo passed. Temporary browser-side tracing then ran the target 12 more times with one worker and concurrent Cargo, starting at load average 8.15: all 12 passed. The toggle changed `scrollHeight` by 250–290px before the first animation-frame sample, but `scrollTop` stayed exactly equal to its pre-click value through 120 animation frames and emitted no scroll event in every traced run—no synchronous or later-layout drift was observed. A final full-order batch kept one Playwright worker, added four PID-tracked CPU burners, and ran six sequential whole suites before stopping on unrelated noise: the first five passed 33/33; in the sixth the Markdown target still passed, then the later macOS tab-strip test timed out with two tabs instead of three. Across all six target executions the original poll callback returned one exactly-equal `scrollTop` sample in 2–8ms, with no unequal value or callback error. The one-minute load average rose from 10.63 to 15.67—above the historical observation despite the smaller burner set—so the unrelated tab failure is not evidence about #1449 either. All temporary tracing, logs, and load processes were removed afterwards. Source inspection still finds one view-only CodeMirror effect and same-transaction projection rebuilds, but neither that shape nor these green traces rule out a rarer browser/layout or test-order failure. The issue's acceptance fork is therefore unsatisfied and #1449 must remain open. No product, test, or test-matrix change is justified by this evidence; weakening equality, raising timeouts, or adding retries would conceal the unanswered question rather than resolve it.

- [2026-08-13] **#933 review round 2: "a completed enumeration did not list it" is weaker evidence than it reads, and the review found the gap in the one place the design says must be *proved*.** J2 enters Missing on disk only when a completed project-files generation *proves* an open document's path is absent, and the card read absence-from-the-generation as that proof. It is not: `project_files::scan_directory` descends exactly one kind of row — a directory it could read — so a directory whose `read_dir` failed is published as a present, `unreadable`-warned, *childless* row, and a symlink/`other`/`unknown` row is listed and never walked. A document under any of those is absent from the enumeration while sitting on disk, and the lifecycle would then pause its autosave and refuse its saves. Fixed with a second reading of the same snapshot (`undescendedPaths`) rather than a new mechanism: the rows the generation listed without walking, whose descendants absence cannot speak about. **The generalisable trap is the obvious generalisation:** "require every ancestor to be listed" sounds stricter and is wrong — a genuinely deleted folder is exactly the case where an ancestor is absent and the documents under it really are gone, so that rule would excuse the deletion it exists to catch. The correct rule is *positive evidence of non-enumeration*, never *absence of evidence of enumeration*. A third way to be unenumerated has no answer in the snapshot at all (a hidden tooling component — `.git`, `node_modules` — produces no row), and mirroring `is_user_visible`'s name list into TS would be the drift CLAUDE.md's cross-boundary rule exists to prevent, so it is #1444 rather than a copied constant.

- [2026-08-13] **#933 review round 2: an intermittent frontend failure was a test asserting into a subtree that had not remounted yet — and the probe that proved it took two minutes after repetition had failed six times.** One full-suite run failed `carries the tab, its buffer, the Missing state, and the backend's open-tab set to a Save As destination`; six isolated runs of that file were green, so repetition was never going to find it. The test waited for the *tab* to appear at the new path and then read `container.querySelector(".cm-content")` — but the tab appears with the rekey while the `EditorShell` under it is remounting and has **no DOM at all** until the destination document's own load resolves, so the query returns `null` and `toHaveTextContent` reports "received value must be a Node". Proved by making that one load land late (a `setTimeout` inside the `openDocument` mock, keyed on the destination path): the old assertion fails deterministically, and the same probe passes once the assertion waits for the condition it asserts. **Two things to carry.** (1) *Any* assertion that reads DOM through a remount boundary needs a condition wait, not a one-shot read — the sibling test three cases down had the identical shape and was fixed with it. (2) When a flake will not reproduce by repetition, stop repeating and **construct the losing order**: the async boundary is usually a mocked promise you already control, and forcing it is cheaper than another twenty runs. The two other intermittents observed the same session (#1448 vitest, #1449 e2e) are *not* claimed to be this class — they were observed under a concurrent `cargo test --workspace` on the same box, which `docs/journal.md` 2026-07-20 (#368) item (4) already records as making arbitrary tests time out, and neither was reproduced, so both are filed with their measurements and no attribution.

- [2026-08-13] **#932 round 3: adding a field to a persisted sidecar is not automatically a `schema_version` bump — bumping can be the data-losing choice.** `store::tab_session` states its own rule ("bumped whenever the on-wire shape changes"), and adding `TabSnapshot.recovered_from` looked like a routine application of it. It is not, because `parse_or_fresh` **deliberately** accepts only files its own exact version wrote (fail-soft restore, CJ ruling #143): a bump does not migrate old sessions, it *discards* them. So bumping would have thrown away every existing user's restored tab set on the first launch after the upgrade — including the capped unsaved buffers of dirty tabs, which is precisely the loss the new field was added to prevent. The asymmetry that decides it: the upgrade direction is certain and universal, the downgrade direction is rare, and reading an older file is unambiguous anyway (a missing `recovered_from` means "an ordinary project tab", which is exactly what every writer of such a file meant, since no build that wrote one could produce a recovery document). Recorded the judgment in `docs/formats/tab-session.md` (CJ asked for it in the doc, not a commit message) with the sharper rule it generalizes to: **bump for a change a reader could misinterpret — a reinterpreted or removed field — not for an addition whose absence already has the right meaning.** Two mechanical notes. (1) The test that pins this is *not* discriminated by the `#[serde(default)]` attribute I first wrote: serde already maps a missing field to `None` for any `Option`, so mutating the attribute away left the test green (vacuous). The attribute was removed and the comment corrected; what the test actually discriminates is the version constant itself — mutating `SCHEMA_VERSION` to `2` reddens it, which is the load-bearing decision. (2) Worth checking any similar "compatibility" attribute for the same redundancy before claiming it in a comment.

- [2026-08-13] **#932 round 3: `cargo test` ran a STALE test binary through a mutation harness, and I believed two false results before catching it.** Verifying whether `#[serde(default)]` on a new `Option` field was load-bearing, a mutate → build → run → restore harness reported the attribute **redundant** (green without it) — then a full `cargo test --workspace` **failed** that same test twice, so I concluded the attribute was load-bearing after all and wrote a code comment about an unexplained `--lib`/`--workspace` divergence. Both readings were wrong. The tell: adding nothing but an `eprintln!` to the failing test — a change that cannot affect behaviour, but *does* force a genuine rebuild — turned the whole suite green. With `cargo clean -p cowrite` before each measurement, the truth is plain and boring: the attribute is redundant (serde defaults a missing `Option`), the code is correct either way, and **every** failure and false-green in that sequence came from cargo reusing a binary compiled from a different revision of the source. The harness wrote and restored the file inside a very short window, and mtime-based freshness did not always notice. Three things to carry: (1) **a Rust mutation check must force a rebuild it can prove** — `cargo clean -p <pkg>`, or at minimum an assertion that the binary changed; a stale binary yields false greens *and* false reds, and the two are indistinguishable from real ones. (2) When a result is scope-dependent for no explicable reason, suspect the build before the language — "feature unification" was a plausible-sounding story I had no evidence for, and I nearly shipped it in a comment. (3) I re-ran all five Rust mutations with clean rebuilds afterwards; all five discriminate genuinely, so only the serde claim was ever an artifact — but I could not have known which without redoing them.

- [2026-08-13] **#932 round 3: `toHaveTextContent` matches substrings, so a "shows the short form" assertion silently passes on the long form that contains it.** The negative cell for the identity-namespace collision asserted that a project document named `untitled:1:notes.md` is labelled `notes.md`. Mutating the classifier back to the path-prefix sniff left that cell **green**: the regression makes the face render the whole identity, and `"untitled:1:notes.md"` *contains* `"notes.md"`. The cell was written specifically to catch that regression and would not have. Anchoring it (`/^notes\.md$/`) reddens it. General shape: whenever the correct value is a **suffix or substring** of the incorrect one — a filename inside a path, a short label inside a long one — `toHaveTextContent`'s default containment makes the assertion inert in exactly the direction that matters. Anchor it, or compare `textContent` exactly. Found only because each new cell was mutation-checked individually rather than as a conjunction.

- [2026-08-12] **#1116 review round 1: a scroll fail-safe and a 1px negative-margin join are mutually exclusive, and no declaration or layout value shows it — only the painted pixels do.** `.tab-bar` carried `overflow-x: auto` as a "the strip scrolls rather than clipping a tab out of reach" fail-safe. `overflow-x: auto` with `overflow-y` unset makes `overflow-y` compute to `auto` as well (CSS Overflow §3), which makes the strip a scroll container, and a scroll container clips its descendants at the **padding box** — on the Windows branch `padding-bottom` is `0` and the active folder tab is pulled exactly 1px past that edge (`margin-bottom:-1px`, `specs/platform-variants.md` "Document tabs"), so the fail-safe removed the one pixel the design specifies and the tab's join to the canvas silently vanished. Nothing observable changed: `getComputedStyle` still reported `margin-bottom: -1px`, and `getBoundingClientRect()` still reported the tab's bottom flush with the strip's border-box bottom, because **clipping alters neither the cascade nor layout geometry — only what reaches the screen.** Two mechanisms for asserting the painted result, one of which does not work: `document.elementFromPoint` at the hairline row is unusable, because the tab is 36.75px tall and Chromium snaps a fractional row to whole pixels — probes from the strip's bottom edge up to 0.9px returned the toolbar *below* the strip, and only at 1.5px did they return the tab, so the very row in question is not addressable by hit-testing. What does work, with no new dependency: `page.screenshot({ clip })` a 1px-wide column through the active tab's last rows, hand the PNG back into the page as a data URL, and decode it via `Image` → `<canvas>` → `getImageData`. That reads the real compositor output — Windows: the whole column is `rgb(252, 251, 248)` (active fill, joined); macOS: the last row is `rgb(227, 223, 214)` (the strip's own hairline, the pill floating 8px clear) — and the A/B is decisive, since reinstating `overflow-x: auto` turns the Windows column's last row into the hairline while macOS, which has no join to lose, stays correctly green. **General rule: when a spec's value is a single pixel of overlap, a declaration test proves only that you wrote it down; ask what could consume it between declaration and screen — clipping, stacking, snapping — and pin the pixel itself. And treat a fail-safe added for an unspecified state as a design change: this one silently traded a specified value for an unspecified one.**

- [2026-08-12] **#1124: "add a CI job" was unbuildable as specified, because the workflow it names is `disabled_manually` — the third card in three weeks to inherit that same false premise.** The card is right that `e2e/acceptance.spec.mjs` had no CI job from the rig landing (#142, 2026-07-15) onward while `docs/test-matrix.md` credited 16 rows to it as `present` — FRs reading as covered by tests that never executed. But `gh api repos/<repo>/actions/workflows` reports `.github/workflows/ci.yml` as `disabled_manually`, so adding `test-e2e` there and calling it a gate would have **reproduced the card's own defect in a new form** — precisely what its item 2 warns against ("a non-blocking job would reproduce this failure in a new form"). This exact rule was already journaled twice: 2026-07-29 (#817) — *"a checked-in lint workflow is not a gate when that workflow is disabled… audit enforcement at the active trigger-to-command edge, not by the presence of a workflow file"* — and 2026-08-07 (#1234), where the same `disabled_manually` fact was asserted wrongly in a card body, inherited verbatim by its Engineer, and survived into a commit message. **A twice-recorded rule did not prevent a third recurrence, because nothing executes it at card-authoring time.** The venue question (self-hosted Windows gate vs. re-enabling hosted CI) is a workload/cost call on CJ's own hardware (#1347) and cannot be settled by an Engineer, so it is filed as #1388 and #1124 is blocked on it; there is also **no live macOS lane at all**, so test-plan §4's "macOS + Windows" is unsatisfiable today by any route. Three things were still landable and venue-independent: the gated subset in one place (`npm run test:e2e:ci`), a `@quarantine` tag whose tracking issue is **mechanically required** by a test, and — the part that actually bites — those shape checks live in `scripts/`, so they run on the live Windows gate via `npm run test:scripts` even while the job they describe is dormant. Two suite defects surfaced only because someone finally ran the thing: a deterministic 358px boundary overflow plus two intermittents (#1382), and a load-sensitive fixture-teardown race (#1383). **General rule: before implementing any card whose deliverable is "make CI do X," resolve the workflow's `state` by execution first — a card that names a file is not evidence that the file runs, and neither is a green local suite.**

- [2026-08-11] **#1375 review round 3: a single sentinel value carrying two meanings keeps reappearing as a new defect no matter which consumer fixes its own reading of it — fix it at the value's own source.** Round 2 correctly made `rolePid: null` confirm a stall (it's workspace-policy.mjs's own documented "no child spawned" shape). What round 2 missed: `defaultActiveDispatches`'s OWN unreadable-marker catch fallback used that EXACT SAME sentinel — `{ role: 'unreadable dispatch marker', rolePid: null, ... }` — for a completely different fact ("I could not even read this marker"). So a genuinely corrupted local marker file (a torn write, a permissions blip) collided with the documented "settled, no child ever spawned" shape and produced the exact false channel-level alert the whole tri-state design exists to prevent — the reviewer executed this directly with a temporarily malformed marker. **The shape of the mistake, stated plainly: round 1 fixed fail-open by making everything unknown; round 2 fixed that by giving `rolePid: null` a confirmed meaning; round 3 finds that the SAME value already had a second, older meaning nobody had reconciled.** Fixed at the reader/core boundary rather than adding a fourth downstream special case: `defaultActiveDispatches`'s unreadable-marker fallback now stamps an explicit `unreadable: true` flag, and `computeRoleActivity` checks it FIRST — before `rolePid` is read for anything — so an unreadable marker always stays `'unknown'` and only a marker that was genuinely read and says no child was ever spawned confirms `'flat'`. Tested at three levels per the reviewer's explicit ask for the REAL reader, not a fake standing in for it: a unit test with a bare `unreadable: true` object, an integration test driving the real `defaultAllActiveDispatches` over a real corrupted marker file into `computeRoleActivity` directly, and an end-to-end test through the composed `defaultCheckStall`. Two Minors in the same round were the same comment/code-mismatch class that has now recurred three consecutive rounds: retention derived from `thresholdMs` when the prose always said "the completeness window" (`minSampleWindowMs`) — invisible in production because the two are always equal there, but a broken contract for any other caller, reproduced with the reviewer's own exact numbers (thresholdMs: 1000, minSampleWindowMs: 3000, 10 flat ticks, stuck `'unknown'` forever); and an injected `isAlive` could fault uncaught, unlike every other injected read dependency, contradicting the module's own "every fault is caught" promise. Did a full holistic pass of the header this time rather than patching the two cited spots alone, and explicitly named `log()` as the one deliberate, reasoned exception to that promise (there is no more-fundamental channel to report a logging failure to) rather than leaving it ambiguous for a fourth round to find. **General rule: when the SAME raw value is read by two different producers of that value for two different meanings — "the domain model's own null" versus "my read of the domain model failed" — collapsing them is not a downstream consumer's mistake to patch each time it resurfaces; it is the value's OWN representation that is broken, and the fix belongs at the point that value is produced, once, not at every place that happens to consume it.**

- [2026-08-11] **#1375 review round 2: "unknown must never confirm a stall" was correct; "therefore almost everything should read unknown" was the overcorrection, and it broke the card's whole purpose.** Round 1's fix for "absence of evidence read as evidence of a stall" pushed so much into `'unknown'` that the detector could barely ever fire, and the reviewer found three NEW Important findings — all introduced by round 1 itself, none pre-existing. The needed shape was three-way, not two: **confirmed working** (never a stall), **confirmed NOT working** (the card's actual signal), **couldn't measure** (unknown, never alerts) — round 1 had collapsed the middle case into the third. (1) A `rolePid: null` marker (workspace-policy.mjs's own documented "no child spawned" shape) and a retained-but-confirmed-dead pid both fell through `isProbablePid`'s silent skip and contributed nothing, when "no subprocess alive" is literally one of #1375's own two stall-confirming facts — fixed by threading an `isAlive` dependency (`defaultProcessAlive`) into `computeRoleActivity` and branching explicitly on both shapes. (2) The CPU-growth verdict window used the SAME cutoff for retention and for the completeness gate, so a sample could be pruned in the exact tick its age first satisfied the gate — at any REAL, non-divisor tick interval (61s against a 1200s/20-minute threshold), no tick ever lands on that single boundary and the gate is never satisfied; **the reviewer executed this and reported the exact numbers: 45 flat-CPU ticks at 60s intervals fired once, 61s intervals fired never, forever.** Fixed by retaining samples for 2x the completeness window and selecting the newest-retained-sample-still-old-enough as a self-correcting reference each tick, turning a single boundary instant into a wide band no realistic jitter can skip. (3) A wiring test for the wiring itself — the wiring line that turns the detector on in production — called the composed function with its own fake `readActiveDispatches`, unaware that `runtime.mjs`'s `{ ...args, readActiveDispatches: defaultAllActiveDispatches }` spread order made the production default unconditionally win, so the call actually reached the REAL cross-worktree registry reader (which can delete stale marker files/directories); it passed only because that registry happened to be empty on the host that wrote it — an environment accident, not a property the test enforced. Fixed by reordering the spread so an explicit override wins (a no-op for every real run, since the real call site never sets that field), and by strengthening the test to use a sentinel value that is NOT indistinguishable from the real reader's own likely output. **General rule: when a reviewer's fix for "X is treated as evidence of Y" over-applies and starts treating almost everything as "unknown," check whether the THIRD state — "confirmed NOT Y" — quietly vanished into the "don't know" bucket along with the genuinely unknown cases; a real tri-state split needs all three states individually reachable, not just the "avoid false positives" state expanded to swallow the others. And test the mutation, not just the intent: several of round 1's own tests injected fake pid numbers with no `isAlive` override and got lucky (or unlucky) based on whatever happened to be running on the machine that wrote them — a unit test of process-state logic must always pin the OS-facing dependency explicitly, never inherit it by default.**

- [2026-08-11] **#1364: an npm provisioning proof is reusable only while the output it attests is still present.** The durable digest from #1351 proves that `npm ci` completed for exact inputs, but an interrupted later `npm ci` can delete `node_modules` without deleting that older digest. Reuse now checks for the worktree's `node_modules` before honoring an exact `provision-npm-ci` proof; absence fails closed to ordinary npm provisioning, while presence preserves #1351's restart saving. This is deliberately an output-existence probe, not an integrity check or an in-progress marker: it catches the absent-output subcase without persisting new state, inspecting dependency contents, or changing cargo proof reuse. A present but partial dependency tree remains outside this mechanism's scope. **General rule: durable evidence for a destructive command needs a cheap live precondition for the output whose continued existence the evidence assumes.**

- [2026-08-10] **#1116: a component whose stylesheet was never written shipped green, because the only suite that renders it applies no stylesheet.** `TabBar` landed on 2026-07-17 (P2a-6, #143/PR #209) with a full WAI-ARIA `tablist` contract, its own keyboard suite, two review rounds, and **zero `.tab-bar` rules in `src/styles.css`** — 24 days green before CJ saw a Windows build and reported it — so every open document laid out as a block-level, full-width filename+`×` row and the "standard tabbed authoring center" `ux-design.md` §4 names was, on screen, a stack of close buttons. Nothing was red: vitest renders in jsdom, jsdom performs no layout and attaches no stylesheet, so the entire class of "the CSS for this component does not exist" is invisible to the frontend suite by construction. **What makes it checkable at unit altitude:** jsdom *does* implement the cascade for `getComputedStyle` — attribute selectors and all — even though it never lays anything out. Attaching the real `src/styles.css` to the test document before rendering therefore turns geometry declarations (`display`, `flex-wrap`, `padding`, `border-radius`, `text-overflow`) into ordinary assertions, and reading the *presence and difference* of colour declarations (active vs inactive) rather than their values sidesteps the one thing jsdom won't do — it leaves `var(--…)` unresolved, returning the literal `var(--color-tab-win-active-bg)` for a set property and `rgba(0, 0, 0, 0)` for an unset one. That asymmetry is usable as a difference assertion and useless as a value assertion; a `border-color: var(…)` shorthand collapses to transparent on *both* sides and silently makes such a cell vacuous, which is why the active/inactive proof here rests on background, weight and ink instead. **Two traps in getting the stylesheet into the test at all,** both of which fail as "empty string" or "wrong URL scheme" rather than as anything self-explanatory: Vitest's default `css: false` stubs a CSS import out to `""` *including* `?raw`, and Vite rewrites the `new URL("./x.css", import.meta.url)` idiom into a dev-server `http://localhost:3000/…` URL that `fileURLToPath` then rejects. `readFileSync(join(dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)), …))` is the one form that survives both. **The real proof still belongs in the browser**, and this is the part worth copying: the e2e cell asserts three tabs share one `top` and three ascending `left`s, and with the stylesheet stashed it reports `[72, 105, 138]` — the reported defect, executed, in the exact shape a user saw it. A pixel claim that can *reproduce* the bug is worth more than one that merely fails. **General rule: a suite that cannot execute a layer cannot vouch for it — so before trusting "the tests are green" on a visual change, ask what the test environment actually runs, and give the un-run layer either a real-browser cell or an explicit gap in the matrix row.**

- [2026-08-10] **#1347: a recommendation whose precondition nobody owns is a decision made by default, filed as if it were still open.** #1340 closed the safety half of the Windows concurrency question and left a conditional — *"do not key it yet; measure runs-per-merged-PR first."* Nothing was wrong with that recommendation except that no card, no cadence, and no owner existed for its antecedent, so it could never evaluate. The record then said "pending" while the behavior said "settled," and the standing cost of that gap is re-litigation: the next agent who notices slow Windows CI and no `concurrency:` block refiles the issue and re-derives the same safety proof. **Decision (CJ, 2026-08-10): the production workflow stays ungrouped.** #982 removed its group outright and no PR-keyed group goes back on top; the absence is a workload-policy decision, not a safety hold. Naming that precisely matters — review round 1 caught this entry's first draft saying "the global group stands," which would have recorded a decision about infrastructure #982 deleted, contradicting `docs/ci-windows-runner.md` and a canary guard that both pin production as ungrouped. The phrase was imported from #1262, whose canary workflow really does have a global group; a sibling card's vocabulary does not survive the move to a workflow with different history. Reopen only on an OBSERVED symptom — Windows CI queue time actually blocking cards on the two-runner pool — never on a schedule and never on the 5-of-100 run rate, which is the wrong denominator (#1262's standing posture). The measurement recipe stays recorded in the #1340 entry for that case. Worth naming what stayed unknown and why it was acceptable: an obsolete run pays a full-history checkout and a cache-free Node setup *before* the freshness check can reject it, so its true runner occupancy is neither 30 seconds nor 15 minutes but a number nobody measured — and #980 having bounded that occupancy well below a full suite is what makes declining to measure it a reasonable call rather than a blind one. **General rule: when you defer a decision on evidence, give the evidence an owner in the same stroke; a conditional recommendation with an unowned antecedent decays into the status quo while still consuming attention as an open question.**

- [2026-08-09] **#1335: a test double for a runner-owned file format has to decode the format like the runner, not like the developer's current shell.** Windows PowerShell 5.1 writes `>> $env:GITHUB_OUTPUT` as UTF-16LE with a BOM, while PowerShell 7 writes UTF-8; reading both through Node's `readFileSync(path, 'utf8')` turns the 5.1 key into BOM mojibake plus embedded NULs, so six positive-value canary cells failed and the two absence cells passed accidentally. The tooling self-test now reads bytes and follows the Actions runner's BOM choices (`FF FE` → UTF-16LE, `EF BB BF` → BOM-stripped UTF-8, otherwise UTF-8), and it probes inbox `powershell` before `pwsh` because that is the edition both Windows workflows actually declare. A real 32-byte UTF-16LE+BOM+CRLF fixture makes the failure host-independent; UTF-8 with and without a BOM keeps the fallbacks honest. Every output-value mismatch includes byte length and a 24-byte hex prefix, turning a recurrence into evidence rather than another host probe. **General rule: when a test emulates a platform parser, fixture the bytes at the parser boundary and select the same runtime the shipped configuration selects; testing through a locally preferred runtime silently replaces the production contract with a workstation accident.**
- [2026-08-09] **#1342: the Windows runner incident exposed a sibling-path normalization gap, but its raw-path producer was already sanitized before this branch began.** Intermediate run 31342962363 at `7ff7afa2` carried an absolute `lstat` path into the extraction scan, leaving the parent `_work\cowrite` segment after #868's exact-root mask. The same PR later merged `f3e1e9ed`, whose `provisioningIdentityErrorLabel` reduces filesystem/tool failures to bounded labels; that sanitizer is already an ancestor of this branch's base, and the live scan therefore sees bare `ENOENT`, not the incident's absolute path. Parent masking is defence-in-depth for any future captured value that legitimately names a sibling path under the runner's environmental work root, not the remedy for that already-closed producer. The serializer masks the exact root first and then the parent, preserving distinct root evidence; a `String.raw` Windows-root/sibling fixture executes that policy on every host. **General rule:** record an incident as evidence for a defensive boundary only after separating the condition it exposed from the source fix that already removed its observed producer.

- [2026-07-29] **A checked-in lint workflow is not a gate when that workflow is disabled, and an unpinned `stable` formatter makes restoring the gate a repo-wide surprise (#817).** The disabled hosted `ci.yml` still described rustfmt, clippy, and test-matrix enforcement, but the only live self-hosted workflow ran none of them; CLAUDE.md and `docs/test-matrix.md` therefore claimed automation that no PR exercised. Rust 1.97.0's rustfmt exposed drift across 28 Rust files, while clippy found three warnings under `--workspace --all-targets -- -D warnings`. The repair puts all three checks in the live Windows workflow, adds the traceability-owning docs to its path filter, and pins Rust 1.97.0 plus rustfmt/clippy in `rust-toolchain.toml`; the disabled workflow names the same version so re-enabling it cannot select a different formatter. General rule: audit enforcement at the active trigger-to-command edge, not by the presence of a workflow file, and make formatter upgrades explicit before treating format as a hard gate. Traces test-plan §4/§9; regression contract: `NI-live-quality-gates`.

## Architectural Decisions

- [2026-10-01] **UXR-Q16.4: a collapsed Timeline expands for runs started outside a persistent comment card (CJ-ratified).** `Address all…`, file-backed selection `Ask agent`, and the same class of run-start actions now reveal the new run and the applicable J13 sent-context line on its owning submitted-turn card at submission. A persistent comment card's `[Address with AI]` keeps its earlier exception: comments stay visible, the card links to the run, and `Unsaved text sent with this run` appears only when unsaved text actually rode the turn. The auto-expansion is caused by the author's action; an arriving J7 permission request and a tab switch do not acquire that behavior. **Scoped UXR-Q2 exception:** preserve a facet that already contains the new run; otherwise select `All` so the run and its applicable J13 disclosure are visible immediately. The ordinary expanded-rail submit still preserves its facet and uses `1 new in Chat →` for a filtered-out card. The comment-card path does not auto-expand or change facets. The visual handoff extends `docs/uxr-q13-visual-refresh-brief.md` for both platform mocks; generated `docs/design/` remains untouched.

- [2026-09-30] **UXR-Q13 / UXR-Q14.3: collapsed Timeline keeps a document-specific comments column in a window-scoped rail mode (CJ-ratified).** A tab switch changes the column's threads or its truthful empty message, not the rail mode or its 400px width. A file-backed pane says `No comments in this document` only after its comments load succeeds with no threads; an untitled pane says `Comments are available after Save As`, because comment attachment is unavailable there. The composer, provider, mic, and J13 standing line remain in expanded Timeline; an Address-with-AI comment card links to its run while the column stays visible and says `Unsaved text sent with this run` only when that text actually rode the turn. This settles the comment-card slice of inherited UXR-Q16.4 without claiming a D31-withheld payload was sent; at-submit disclosure for other composer-less collapsed routes remains open. Appearance is handed off in `docs/uxr-q13-visual-refresh-brief.md`, outside generated `docs/design/`.

- [2026-08-19] **Blind time is judged where the evidence is read, not where it is produced — #1765 settled with no second watchdog abort, and a measured Stage 3 acceptance row instead (#1769, CJ-ratified).** #1762 made an acceptance window's unreadable-authority time visible (`supervision.cumulativeUnreadableMs`) and nothing judged it, so the open question was whether a CUMULATIVE bound should end a window the way the SUSTAINED streak does. It should not, and the measurement is what says so: cumulative blind time grows linearly with window length at a constant flap rate, so any absolute duration is eventually crossed by a healthy-enough authority on a long-enough window — at a steady 9.4% blind fraction the 86.4-minute sustained budget is passed ~19 hours into a 144-hour window whose authority is 90.6% readable with detection latency never worse than ~2 poll intervals. That is #1762's own defect rebuilt: destroying days of unattended real-spend evidence to report a fault worth seconds. A FRACTION does not have the property, and read at the boundary it destroys nothing, so the judgement moved to Stage 3 acceptance as `measures.supervision.blindTimeFraction` with a ratified 5% ceiling. **Three things generalise past this card.** (1) *The ceiling is a relationship, not a constant*: 5% is five times #1762's sustained budget (1% of the window) worth of blind time once spread rather than concentrated — a number derived from an existing bound can be re-derived when that bound moves, where a free constant can only be re-argued. (2) *Name the unit or measure the wrong thing*: 43% of authority samples failed in the fixture the ceiling was chosen against while only 9.4% of wall time was blind, a ~4.5x gap on ONE behaviour, because a defeated sample costs its own ~15-second retry ladder rather than a whole 60-second poll interval. Both numbers are now published, the criterion is on TIME, and the sample fraction carries no pass/fail status at all — #1716's lesson applied to a unit rather than to a mechanism. (3) *A criterion nothing evaluates is the #1716 defect wearing new clothes*, so this one is emitted as a pass/fail row by the same reporter that emits the other acceptance measures, and a window with no watchdog evidence reads `unmeasured` rather than passing by silence. **The divisor is where this card could most easily have shipped a wrong number**: elapsed supervised time, never the registered window length, because #1716's `thresholds-met` completion routinely ends a 144-hour registration in hours — and a single fixture cannot tell the two divisors apart, since any one value is consistent with SOME blind total. Only sweeping the elapsed axis at a constant blind fraction separates them: the measured value must not move when the window length does. **Round 1 added the corollary the card's own wording implied and the code missed:** a health record cannot tell *clean* from *never looked*. `evaluateLocalWindowTick` can end a window on tick 0 — a missing lock, a PID replacement — and `settle()` then publishes the INITIAL health record verbatim, so a window nothing supervised reports a perfectly honest `cumulativeUnreadableMs: 0`, and `0 / elapsed` is a real number that read `pass`. The floor was already in the diff, on the measure that does **not** gate (`measureFailedSampleFraction` guards its own numerator with exactly this reasoning) and missing from the one that does. The generalisable form: **when a measure's numerator can be legitimately zero, the denominator is not the only thing to guard — ask what a zero numerator means when nothing was ever sampled, and put the floor on the measure that gates.**

- [2026-08-18] **Document admission needs two explicit gates: source safety is necessary, and a supported editing/projection contract is independently necessary (D27/FR-ED-9, #1720).** CJ opened the app on this repo and asked for Markdown-only, then immediately supplied the counterexamples: `.txt` belongs; `.html` does not “until I add a render”; code never belongs. The first proposal tried to make one hidden invariant explain all three — pending revisions live in the source as CriticMarkup (D5/FR-ED-4, FR-X-8), so code/config/data can be corrupted by document writes — but the renderer examples disprove the word *all*: `.rst` can round-trip safely and still be a dishonest Markdown editing experience. The durable rule is a conjunction. **A file type is a Cowrite document only when CriticMarkup is safe throughout it AND Cowrite explicitly supports how that type is edited and projected.** The transferable lesson is to distrust a claimed single invariant when the decision's own exception list invokes a second one. Also: verify decision IDs at source — D1 governs portable skills, not tracked changes.

- [2026-08-18] **A discovery-driven surface needs both entity-shaped rows and an explicit ownership rule; “not a file tree” alone does not prevent duplicate paths (#1720).** FR-SKILL-5 finds skills by `SKILL.md` at any depth and workflows by a configurable `*.md` glob. That makes a location-rooted Skills tree wrong, but simply placing discovery beside Documents creates a different ambiguity: a non-dot `claude_skills/x/SKILL.md` qualifies for both sections, and a workflow is a file rather than the directory-shaped entity a skill is. A configurable workflow glob creates one more overlap when it matches a file inside a skill root. J2 therefore uses one precedence order: a discovered workflow owns its file; otherwise the deepest discovered skill owns its directory material; Documents owns the remaining eligible paths.
