Reaching for the exit table's note here would have invented a J4 delta out of a row that explicitly does not cover this case.

- [2026-08-31] **#2153 separates fatal evidence from terminal authority at the detached Windows worker boundary.** A worker that has won an exact request claim can fail after its ignored descriptors make ordinary stderr unavailable, so its safe durable record is a bounded `state/diagnostic.json` bound to dispatch/request/owner/PID/birth identity and lifecycle phase. The client may surface that redacted reason and artifact path, plus the independently observed exit code or signal, but neither the artifact nor PID death can become `result.json`, terminal proof, retry authority, or workflow authority. Malformed, oversized, absent, and identity-mismatched diagnostics remain fail-closed. A real detached descendant-survival witness proves the worker can exit, retain actionable evidence, and leave terminal result absent.

- [2026-08-28] **A `[\s\S]{0,N}` window says "within N characters", and the only mechanical tell that it is standing in for "in one passage" is whether its matched span crosses a paragraph boundary (#1670).** `maintenance-runbook.test.mjs` pinned `provenance.attribution` → `windowRuns` → `selectedRunId` document-wide inside `{0,600}`. Nothing about the regex distinguishes it from the 43 other bounded pins in the same file, and the argument that saved it during #1660 — the two OUTER anchors, `provenance.attribution` and `selectedRunId`, occur exactly once each, so they pin the match and no wider window can reach a coincidental distant pair (the middle anchor `windowRuns` occurs four times and is pinned only by them) — is a *soundness* argument that says nothing about *stability*: the bound had already been widened 500 → 600 when a 24-character clause pushed the measured span from 483 to 507, and 93 characters of slack remained. Reading the file by eye cannot separate the pin that has to be widened whenever the prose grows from the pin that never will. Instrumenting it can: patching `assert.match`/`assert.doesNotMatch` in a `--import` preload, re-running the file, and recording for each bounded regex its *measured* gap per window, its anchors' occurrence counts, and whether the matched span contains a blank line yields the whole census in one run. Of 44 bounded pins in that file, **exactly one** — the defective one — had a span crossing a paragraph break; every other span resolved inside a single paragraph, where a character window and "co-located" mean the same thing. **The rule that falls out: a bounded window whose match must cross a paragraph boundary is not expressing proximity, it is approximating passage membership, and it should extract the passage (`admissionClause`/`attributionPassage`) and assert inside it instead.** Two design points the rework turned up. First, the passage's start marker must stop SHORT of any field the pins assert (`'Every report states its attribution under'`, not `'…under `provenance.attribution`'`) — a marker containing an asserted field is deleted along with that field, so the cell reddens at the extraction rather than at the assertion that owns the field, and the per-field non-vacuity proof proves nothing about the pin. Second, the proof that a passage-scoped pin actually beats the document-wide one it replaced is not "delete the field": it is **delete the field from the passage and re-add the identical clause elsewhere in the document**. Measured across all eight mutated documents, the retired ORDERED pin reddens on most of them too — it was never a plain presence check, so "the retired form would still pass this" is a claim to measure rather than assume — but a document-wide PRESENCE check on the field is green on every relocated document while the passage pin reddens, and `windowRuns` needs no relocation at all to show it: it occurs three further times in the runbook, so deleting it from the passage alone already produces a document where "the field is in the document" is true and "the field is in the passage" is false. Non-vacuity and passage-scoping fall out of one mutation.

- [2026-08-28] **An unhandled promise rejection can be the ONLY thing that reddens a test suite for a real defect, which makes "add a `.catch()`" the wrong fix even when the swallow is genuine (#1701, following #1690).** `externalChange.ts`'s `createExternalChangePoller` does `void check().finally(...)` — `void` discards the `.finally()` promise, but that promise still rejects whenever `check()` rejects, so a rejecting check becomes an unhandled rejection: silent to the *application* (no `onError`, no caller, nothing renders), but loud to vitest, which fails a run over it even when every individual test reports passed (#1690's exact signature: `1315/1315 passed`, exit 1). #1701 was filed because `EditorShell.tsx`'s `checkDiskForExternalChange` left its own tail — everything past the supersession guard — with no `try`/`catch` at all, so a throw there escaped through that same discarded promise with no consumer to report it to. The instinctive fix is to catch it and call `onErrorRef.current?.(error)`; that is wrong on its own, because in this codebase `onError` is optional and the great majority of call sites (113 of 119 in `EditorShell.test.tsx` at the time — only 6 pass one, per the Reviewer's per-test-body scan) mount with none — `?.()` on `undefined` is a silent no-op, which trades one silent failure mode for a narrower one and **spends the loud channel that is the only reason #1690's fixture defect was ever caught**. The shipped shape keeps both properties: with a real `onError` consumer, report once per run of consecutive failures (a dedicated ref, reset only when a check completes normally — not scoped to a Missing *episode* the way a sibling ref is, since a tail throw has no episode to belong to); with none, deliberately re-throw and let it escape exactly as before, now documented as the poller's own contract rather than an accident of `void`. **The general rule: before silencing an unhandled rejection, check whether its loudness is doing real work — a swallow that only ever fires in a codepath nothing actually consumes converts a visible test failure into an invisible production one, which is a worse trade than the "bug" it fixes.** A second consumer of the same tail needed its own, narrower fix: `saveNow` awaits that tail directly (via `pollNowRef.current()`, not through the poller's own `.finally()` chain), so an unconsumed rejection there would reject `saveNow()` and, through it, `EditorShellHandle.save()` — which `TabbedEditor.tsx`'s dirty-close guard awaits with no `try`/`catch` of its own, so it would neither close the tab nor report anything. `saveNow` now wraps that one await locally and falls through to the refusal (`return false`) it already had for the sibling case where the read itself fails outright.

- [2026-08-19] **A restart test can pass vacuously because the *first* session it compares against is not a fresh one — the module registry's prior state is itself a confound (#1728).** The defect was `timeline/types.ts`'s `nextId`, a module-scoped counter that resets with the process, so card ids persisted to the D15 transcript (`project-grounding-1`, eight times in CJ's own store) repeat across launches. The card was explicit that the load-bearing test must cross a process boundary, and `vi.resetModules()` + a re-import is that boundary in vitest. The first version did exactly that — mint from the module, `vi.resetModules()`, mint again, assert disjoint — and it **passed against the unfixed counter**, because the first mint came from this test file's *statically imported* instance whose counter the 20-odd earlier tests in the file had already advanced past 20; the fresh instance started at 1 and overlapped nothing. Resetting the registry **before** the first session too (so both are pristine launches, which is what the bug actually is) turned it red. **The general rule: a test that simulates "restart" has two sessions, and it is only a restart test if *both* of them start from the state a restart produces. Compare a fresh instance against an advanced one and you are testing the counter's monotonicity, not its reuse.** Found only by running the new test against the unfixed code before writing the fix — the same per-assertion mutation check the 2026-08-14 entries above arrive at from the other direction. Same tranche: the `claimed`-id guard in the read-time repair was initially uncovered (its mutation stayed green, because the fixture happened to order the pre-claimed id before the duplicate), and needed a fixture whose ordering makes the guard the only thing standing between the repair and an id it would steal.

- [2026-08-16] **A `useRef` that participates only in one effect's own closures is scoped to the wrong lifetime, and the leak only shows up across two mounts of the same component (#1599, PR #1639 review).** `EditorShell`'s save callback set `ownPublicationRef.current` (a component-level ref) before its `await saveDocument(...)`, so a poll could recognize Cowrite's own in-flight publication and not misread it as an external change. Every other participant in that same save/poll flow — `cancelled`, `runSave`, `checkDiskForExternalChange`, `scheduler` — was already a `let` local to the `[root, path]` mount effect, freshly bound on every remount; only this one ref was shared across remounts, reset to `null` at the top of each new effect run. That reset closes the obvious window but not the real one: a trailing write coalesced under the *old* document identity, left queued past unmount, resumes and re-sets the shared ref to the old document's content *after* the new effect's reset already ran — so the new document's poll can read a live document B's disk bytes as "my own publication" when they happen to coincide with stale document A's, suppressing a genuine FR-ED-7 divergence for B. Reviewer reproduced it by three runs (base passes, head fails, deleting the compensating `cancelTrailing()` line alone breaks one of thirteen cells) before diagnosing the ref's scope as the actual defect — the removed line was hiding the leak by cancelling the class of trailing work that could trigger it, at the cost of also dropping legitimate final edits on documents with no divergence at all, which is out of FR-ED-7's own stated scope ("while the choice is unresolved"). Fix: make `ownPublication` a plain closure-local like its siblings, needing no explicit reset (a fresh `let` binding is what every effect run already gets), which makes the cross-document leak structurally impossible rather than merely covered by an unrelated side effect. **The general rule: when a piece of mutable state exists only to serve a group of closures that already live inside one effect run, give it the same lifetime as the rest of that group — a shared ref among effect-locals is exactly the seam where "the second mount" reads state "the first mount" is still writing.**

- [2026-08-15] **A pause implemented as "consult the state at the moment of acting" ends the instant that state ends — which is one event too early whenever the state's *end* is the dangerous moment (#1595).** J2 pauses autosave while a document's file is missing, and `EditorShell` implemented that as `triggerSave` reading `missingRef` at fire time, with an explicit comment that this is why "autosave needs no resuming beyond that ... the pause is `missingRef` being consulted at save time rather than a stopped timer." That is a genuinely good property for *entering* the state, and exactly wrong for leaving it: the path an absent file leaves behind is free, so what occupies it next may be a concurrent creator's file, and the first write after the state clears lands on bytes nobody has read. The reported symptom was a Windows E2E case whose disk held Cowrite's retained buffer instead of a concurrent creator's — user-visible data loss against FR-ED-7 and TD-2's source of truth. **The transferable shape: when a guard exists because some fact is unknown, its lifetime is "until the fact is known", not "while the flag that made it unknown is set" — and those two differ by exactly the window in which the flag clears but nothing has re-established the fact.** The repair makes the pause outlast the missing state, lifted only by a completed read of the path (or by a Recreate that published bytes Cowrite chose, which establishes it without reading).
  - **The issue's stated cause was right about the mechanism and wrong about its location, and only measurement separated them.** It named `handleRecreate`'s `reappeared` branch calling `onMissingResolved()` before its asynchronous FR-ED-7 check completes. Driving the *automatic* reappearance instead — the ordinary rail refresh, where neither `handleRecreate` nor `onMissingResolved` runs at all — reproduced the same loss on macOS, three times out of three. So the await ordering inside that one branch was a symptom of the pause's shape, not the cause; a fix aimed at that branch would have left the automatic path, which every platform has, still losing bytes. Worth the twenty minutes: the falsifying experiment here was "run the other route", and it moved the fix from one branch to the invariant.
  - **The e2e reproduction's flakiness was a measured coin flip, not bad luck.** With both D23 clocks at 50ms, the interval between the last keystroke and the missing state clearing measured 45ms and 51ms across two runs — against a 50ms debounce. That is why the case failed twice in a burn-in and passed on retry, and it is why the permanent e2e regression sets the two clocks *apart* (a 2s debounce, a 60s poll) so the ordering under test is the one that happens rather than one to wait for. An injectable clock is not only a way to avoid sleeping; it is a way to make a race deterministic in the direction you are testing.
  - **Retries turned a reproduction into a green suite.** Both cited runs reported `1 flaky, 46 passed` under `retries: 2`, so the failure was visible in the log and invisible in the result. Evidence that a race repair holds cannot come from a lane that retries — hence a dispatch-only mode in the registered Windows workflow that runs the focused case repeatedly with `--retries=0` and fails on any single iteration. Keeping the mode in an already default-branch-registered workflow makes it executable on an unmerged ref; its separately named operator job remains outside both the standing required-check policy and #1403's in-flight burn-in sample.

- [2026-08-14] **A classifier's own dispatch is a separate claim from its shape functions, and "in-regex" is not "modelled" (#1441 review round 1, PR #1441 on #1248).** `PRODUCER_PATH` matched six `(area, form)` combinations by construction (2 areas x 3 forms), but only four were ever meant to route anywhere; the dispatch (`if (area === 'data-reader') return attributeDataReader(...)`) routed ALL SIX, so `data-reader/<stream>.offset` silently reached `attributeDataReader`'s snapshot-form branch and was attributed on the SIBLING `snapshot.json`'s self-consistency alone — the file that actually changed contributed nothing to the verdict. The negative test that should have caught it (`a watched path outside the four producer shapes is never attributed`) only drove a path the regex refuses on SHAPE (one directory too deep); it never exercised a combination the regex accepts but the dispatch was never taught. General rule, cheap to apply next time: when a matcher and a dispatcher are two different pieces of code (a regex plus a series of `if`s keyed off part of the match), enumerate the matcher's OWN combinatorial space and write a negative cell for every combination the dispatcher does not explicitly name — "the regex refuses it" and "the dispatcher refuses it" are different claims, and only a cell built from the SECOND one proves the second one.
- [2026-08-14] **A test double that echoes back whatever the fixture just wrote is a legitimate isolation choice for the OTHER ~30 tests in a file, but it is also, on its own, "a fake with no failure mode" for the ONE thing it stands in for — the two readings coexist and neither excuses skipping the other (#1441 review round 1, Minor 2).** Closing a pid-reuse window meant comparing a lock's recorded `startTime` against the pid's real OS start time. Injecting that OS probe would have forced ~30 unrelated `#1248` guard tests (append rules, checkpoint bounds, data-reader digest, …) to each construct a real, platform-specific OS reading just to stay green, so the fixture's default probe instead reads back whatever `startTime` the SAME fixture's own lock file currently holds — trivially, unconditionally true, and portable to Windows CI for free. That default is exactly right for every test that is not about the OS-agreement gate itself, and exactly wrong (vacuous) as a claim about the gate's own discrimination — so the gate got its OWN dedicated tests that either use the REAL default probe against a REAL running process (the "could a fake even represent this" check) or explicitly inject a MISMATCHED value to prove the gate refuses it. A repo-wide "always use the real thing" or "always inject" rule would have been wrong either way; the question is which claim a given test is making, cell by cell.
- [2026-08-14] **The dedicated pid-reuse test built in the entry directly above this one was ITSELF vacuous in exactly the way it warns against — a single injected value applied SYMMETRICALLY across a two-sided check cannot tell "both sides discriminate" from "one side does" (#1441 review round 2, PR #1441 on #1248, second finding on this card that a per-assertion claim did not survive being measured).** `liveProducer` checks `osStartMatchesLock` at BOTH the before-edge and the after-edge of the bracket, because the two edges close different windows — before catches an already-stale lock, after is the ONLY thing that catches a pid recycled DURING the bracket. Round 1's test injected one probe returning ONE mismatched value for every call, so it was received identically at both edges: deleting EITHER check alone left the OTHER check still seeing the same mismatch and still refusing the change, so the suite stayed fully green (62/62) with either check removed — measured, not assumed, with the applied patch confirmed via a `grep` count of the check before each run. Only the CONJUNCTION was covered, and the commit/matrix claimed otherwise ("checked at BOTH ends... mutation-proven", "each mutation reddens exactly its own cell") — a claim that was false and shipped as fact. Fix: `observe()` calls the probe exactly ONCE per bracket edge (before, then after `mutate()` runs), so a probe that COUNTS ITS OWN CALLS can answer differently at each edge — two new cells, one agreeing-then-disagreeing and its mirror, each reddening only when ITS OWN check is dropped. **The general and now twice-learned rule: "checked at both ends" (or any N-sided invariant) is a claim about N independent code paths, and a single symmetric input can only ever exercise their conjunction — proving each side needs an input shaped to differ BETWEEN the sides, not just an input that is wrong on both.** When a fixture-level double is built to be intentionally trivial for the tests that are not about the property under test (the entry above), audit the SAME double again for whether it is ALSO trivial in a way that hides asymmetric discrimination for the tests that ARE about it.
- [2026-08-07] **"The failing thing will report itself" is a premise, not a fact — check that the failing thing ever executes (#1229).** #1229 asked a displaced canary run to emit `skipped — displaced by a newer canary`, and called it "the minimum regardless" of which concurrency fix was chosen. It is unimplementable as written. A run evicted from a concurrency group is cancelled while **pending**: `GET /actions/runs/<id>/jobs` returns `total_count: 0` for all eight of the 2026-08-07 cancellations, so there is no job, no step, no `if: cancelled()` hook and no token-bearing context to call the API from. The outcome is reachable only from **outside** the run — a `workflow_run` observer on `ubuntu-latest`, which is what shipped. **Eviction, OOM-kill and queue-timeout all share this shape**, and the honest fix for all three is an external observer; the tell is that the reporting code lives inside the thing whose non-execution is the event being reported. Two design notes that generalise: the observer must run on a *hosted* runner, because an observer that queues for the same starved pool it observes is subject to the failure it is reporting; and its API read must only name the *cause*, never establish the *fact* — the run being cancelled comes from the event payload, so an unreadable job list still publishes an `undetermined` verdict rather than leaving the head bare, since "absent reads as green" is the whole reason the observer exists. Two smaller traps re-encountered while building it: `assert.doesNotMatch(yaml, /self-hosted/)` failed on the **comment explaining why the observer is not self-hosted** (#1234's second-order trap, now twice), so the assertion parses `runs-on:` lines and compares the set; and `check-test-matrix.mjs` silently failed to bind two rows because their test titles used `'…run\'s head'` — **#1239's escaped-quote extraction bug, hit in the wild**, which is worth fixing precisely because its failure mode is a row that looks present and binds nothing. **Caught at review, and it is the sharpest form of the same lesson:** the observer's own `permissions:` block named `actions: read` and `checks: write` and omitted `contents`. Naming any scope sets every unnamed one to `none`, and this repo is private, so `actions/checkout` would have failed on step 1 — the observer would never have run, and the displaced head would have stayed bare, which is the exact outcome it was built to prevent. **It would also have hidden itself:** a `workflow_run` run reports against the default branch, so its red status never lands on the pull request whose head went unreported. A monitor that fails silently is worse than no monitor, because its absence is now read as "nothing to report" — so the guard has to assert the monitor's own preconditions, not just its logic. Nine mutations of the observer's behaviour had all been killed and not one of them could have caught this, because every one of them mutated code rather than the *capability grant the code needs to execute at all*.

- [2026-08-07] **A rendezvous built on artifacts is not a rendezvous: the artifact list outlives the job, so the barrier passed against a DEAD peer and the isolation canary went GREEN having measured nothing (#1229/#1231).** Both filed issues describe the canary being *wrong loudly* (#1231, false red) or *absent* (#1229, evicted). Investigating them turned up a third face nobody filed, and it is the worst one: `Isolation canary` reporting **success**. Mechanism — the barrier polled `GET /actions/runs/<id>/artifacts` for `$ready.Count -ge 2`, but a run's artifact list is **durable for the whole run**; `isolation-ready-<peer>` stays listed after that peer job exits. Measured on two real runs: in `31046727724` (2026-08-05) `head` burned its 450s wait and failed at 21:08:39; `base` then started at **21:08:41 on the same runner** (`cowrite-windows-2`), cleared the barrier in **zero seconds** (21:09:06 → 21:09:06) against head's 21:00:43 artifact, probed a `RUNNER_WORKSPACE` nobody else was using, and passed. `30684554436` (2026-08-01) is byte-for-byte the same shape. Two jobs, one runner, strictly sequential, zero overlap — and a green check asserting work-root isolation. **The general trap: a liveness question answered with a durable artifact is answered wrong, and it fails in the SAFE-LOOKING direction, so it never shows up as a flake.** #1231's own recommendation (poll `runs/<id>/jobs` as well) turns out to be the fix for this too — the peer must be `in_progress` **and** both markers visible, since artifacts alone prove the peer *once* reached the publish step and job status alone cannot tell whether it has got there yet. Second-order lesson about the guard: the pre-existing test asserted `$ready.Count -ge 2`, `isolation-ready-base`, `isolation-ready-head`, the bounded loop and the artifacts URL — every one of them non-vacuous, and **all of them still matched the broken code**, because the text was never what was wrong. The tests added here execute the workflow's own `run:` block (lifted out of the YAML, never copy-pasted) under PowerShell against a scripted pool, and the stale-peer case is asserted by *exit code*: it exits 0 at the pre-fix head and 1 after. **When a guard pins the text of a rendezvous, ask what STATE the rendezvous can be in that the text cannot see.** **Coda, from this card's own first live run (`31206416568`), which is why the fix shipped with a liveness *threshold* and not just a liveness *check*:** `head` hit the stale-peer case exactly as designed and correctly reported not-measured where the old code would have passed vacuously — but `base` misclassified the *same* run as an artifact-visibility fault, i.e. blamed the barrier, because it read "peer is `in_progress`" as "peer has had time to publish". The peer had got a runner **14 seconds** before base's budget expired after 8.5 minutes queued behind a production suite; it was live and had published nothing, which is starvation, not a broken barrier. **`in_progress` is not `has-had-time-to-act`** — a liveness predicate needs a *duration* before absence of evidence becomes evidence, and the safe direction is generous (a genuine fault reported as not-measured still fails the job; starvation reported as a fault blames the diff for the pool). Threshold set at 24 polls (~120s) against a measured ~17-27s publish latency and ~15-20s checkout. The general shape: **a fix for a false-green race is itself a race, and the first live run under real contention is the only thing that tells you which side of the boundary you landed on.**

- [2026-08-07] **Two defect shapes a reviewer found in my own fix for #1229/#1231, both of which turn a verdict into a lie rather than a crash.** (1) **Accumulated evidence has to outlive the thing it is evidence about.** The barrier-fault check read `if ($peerStatus -eq 'in_progress' -and $peerLivePolls -ge 24)` — it gated a *history* (24 polls of observed liveness) behind a *current state*. But a genuinely broken barrier times BOTH slots out at roughly the same moment, so whichever slot noticed its peer terminate first re-labelled a real fault as `"had already finished... a runner-pool outcome, not an isolation violation"` — a neutral excuse. Reproduced by execution: a peer live for 50 polls that publishes nothing and then exits was excused. **Roughly half of every real barrier break would have been excused**, and the failure direction is the dangerous one — a fault downgraded to "nothing was measured". The fix is to drop the current-status conjunct entirely: 120s of observed liveness with no marker is a barrier fault whether or not the peer is still there. **When a predicate combines "what I have seen" with "what is true now", check whether the subject can exit between the two — and note that this is exactly the same class as the durable-artifact bug the card was created to fix, re-introduced one level up.** (2) **An unguarded API read inside a loop whose job is to produce a verdict destroys the verdict.** Both `Invoke-RestMethod` polls were bare; injecting one 502 on the 5th of 90 polls aborted the step with a raw PowerShell stack trace, **no check-run, no reason** — which this canary's own documentation instructs operators to read as a real isolation fault. Guarding both with `try/catch → $null` (a failed read is *not information about the peer*) costs one round and keeps the verdict. Two things fell out: a slot that never once read the job list must not claim "the peer never got a runner" — it never looked, which is a fourth, distinct not-measured cause; and consulting the artifact list only while the peer is live took this step's poll volume from 180 back to **90 per slot** (measured), restoring the timeout margin the liveness check had halved. **A step that can fail closed with no artifact is worse than one that fails loudly, whenever an absent artifact is itself meaningful to a reader.**

- [2026-08-07] **A workflow run cancelled while PENDING creates no job, so an evicted run cannot report its own eviction — #1229's filed "option 3" is dead as written (#1229).** #1229 proposes, as the "minimum regardless" fix, that a displaced canary emit a check-run reading `skipped — displaced by a newer canary`. It cannot. All 8 of the 2026-08-07 cancellations return `total_count: 0` from `GET /actions/runs/<id>/jobs` — the run was evicted from the concurrency group before a job was ever created, so there is no step to run, no `if: cancelled()` to hook, and no token-bearing context to call the API from. (One older cancellation, `30647987352`, *does* show steps — it was cancelled **in progress**, a different case that is easy to mistake for a counterexample.) The outcome #1229 wants is reachable only from **outside** the run: a `workflow_run`-triggered workflow on `ubuntu-latest`, which the repo does not have today. **Before designing around "the failing thing will report itself," check that the failing thing ever gets to execute** — an eviction, an OOM kill and a queue timeout all share this shape, and the honest fix is always an external observer. Related measurement correction: the retention window `gh run list --limit 300` reaches back to **2026-07-31 (266 runs)**, not the 119 both prior measurements sampled; the cancellation burst recurs on 07-31, 08-02 and 08-07 rather than being one 37-minute window, but only **5 of 266 runs (1.9%)** left a head with no canary verdict at all — the other 36 were same-branch self-eviction of a superseded head, which per-ref grouping would not remove either. **A burst that recurs three times in eight days is a different animal from a one-day artifact, and the last-N window hides both the recurrence and the fact that most of the count is benign.**

- [2026-08-07] **Non-vacuity and sufficiency are different questions, and a mutation test answers only the first (#1234/PR #1237).** The guard added for #1234 was `assert.doesNotMatch(pathsBlock, /docs\/test-matrix\.md/)`. It was **genuinely non-vacuous** — two independent agents proved every assertion independently load-bearing, and proved the block-capture regex fail-closed, so deleting `paths:` entirely (the worst regression) failed loudly. It was still **insufficient**: adding `- 'docs/**'` alongside the three machinery paths reproduces #1234's defect exactly — the canary fires on every card again, since every card edits `docs/test-matrix.md` — and the test passed green. A guard that pins a **literal string** does not pin the **property**. The fix was to parse the block's entries and assert **exact set equality** against the expected set, so any addition fails and the diagnostic names the unexpected entry. **After proving a guard fires on the exact defect, ask the separate question: what ELSE expresses this defect?** A second-order trap fell out of the same shape — `doesNotMatch` read the captured block *including comments*, so documenting the exclusion inline in the YAML (which #1234's own scope asked for) made the test fail; stripping comment lines before comparison closed it. Adversarial re-review of the set-equality form ran 31 workflow-level mutations — 18/18 guard bites, 8/8 documented allowances, and 5 characterization probes — plus 18 direct parser probes and found **no silent-drop vector** — every corruption lands as an *extra* entry, so the guard over-reports rather than under-reporting, which is the safe direction for a set-equality assertion.

- [2026-08-07] **An unverified infrastructure claim in a card body is inherited by the Engineer and ends up in `main`'s permanent history (#1234/PR #1237).** #1234 as first written asserted `matrix:check` "runs on every PR in three places," naming `windows-self-hosted.yml`, `ci.yml`, and `windows-runner-contention-benchmark.yml`. **Exactly one runs on pull requests:** the Actions API reports `ci.yml` as `disabled_manually`, and the contention benchmark is `on: workflow_dispatch:` only. The conclusion survived — the one that runs is the required check — but the margin was thinner than stated, and the error propagated: the Engineer carried the parenthetical verbatim into its commit message, and on a **single-commit branch a squash merge makes that message `main`'s permanent body**, where it would have been the last surviving copy of the claim (the committed `docs/ci-windows-runner.md` already said it correctly, singular). Cost: one adversarial pass to catch it in the card, one review round to catch it in the commit message, and a third head. **Verify claims about CI/infrastructure topology by execution before an Engineer inherits them** — `gh api repos/<repo>/actions/workflows` takes seconds and answers "does this actually run" directly. Sharper version: the fact was already in this session's own memory (`ci.yml` disabled since 2026-07-10, corrected 2026-07-27) and was contradicted anyway. **A remembered fact is worth nothing if the claim is not checked against it at the moment of writing.**

- [2026-08-07] **A cancelled workflow binds no check-run, so "unverified" and "inapplicable" are the same API response — observed live on the PR that reduces the very trigger causing it (#1229/#1234).** At PR #1237's head `fbab9b47`, `GET /commits/<sha>/check-runs` returned `total_count = 1` (the Windows suite alone). The canary run for that head (`31199626559` evicting `31199424948`) was `completed/cancelled`, displaced ~3 minutes later by a queued canary from an unrelated branch under #1229's global concurrency group. The two immediately preceding heads of the **same branch** (`80ab2340`, `27622369`) both ran the canary to `success` on an applicable file set — the control that distinguishes eviction from a path-filter skip. Any consumer asking "are the checks that exist green?" answers **yes** on a head whose canary never ran. It was resolvable here only by a step no automated consumer could take: `fbab9b47` is a message-only amend over `27622369` with a byte-identical tree (`33e2177c`, confirmed by `git diff-tree`), and the canary had passed on that tree. **Treat absent as not-green, and when reasoning around an absence, say out loud which artifact actually carries the verdict** — here, a tree hash rather than a SHA.

- [2026-08-05] **`'PATH' in env` is false on Windows, so asserting env inheritance by naming one variable is a host-dependent test (#1166).** A test asserted that the dispatch env still merges onto `process.env` rather than replacing it, and proved it with `assert.equal('PATH' in s.env, true)`. Green on macOS, red on the Windows self-hosted runner: Windows spells the variable **`Path`**, and the dispatch env is a spread copy (`{...process.env}`) that preserves the host's real casing — `process.env.PATH` works there only because Node proxies `process.env` case-insensitively, and a spread copy is a plain object with no such proxy. **The defect was in the test, not the product** — the budget merges correctly on both platforms — but it would have broken `main`, and it shipped in the first commit and survived a review whose cross-platform lens correctly cleared the three env knobs as platform-neutral without looking at the assertion *around* them. Fixed by asserting on the whole key set (`for (const key of Object.keys(process.env)) assert.ok(key in s.env)`), which is both platform-neutral by construction and a stronger statement of what "merges, never replaces" actually claims. **Generalizable: any assertion that hard-codes one environment variable name is a portability bug waiting for CI to find it** — assert the property (every key survives), not an instance of it. Corollary worth keeping: a spread of `process.env` silently loses Node's case-insensitivity on Windows, so `{...process.env}` and `process.env` are not interchangeable there.

- [2026-08-05] **After a squash merge, continuing on the merged branch turns the next PR into a revert of everything that landed in between (#1166 → #1181/#1186).** #1181 was squash-merged and GitHub auto-deleted the branch. Two follow-up commits later, `git diff origin/main HEAD --stat` on that same branch reported **1,951 deletions across 27 files** — not my change, but the undoing of #1178/#1180 and #1175/#1179, which had merged in the interim. The mechanism is that a squash commit is *not an ancestor* of the branch it came from, so the branch's tree is simply "main as it was, plus my edits", and diffing it forward proposes reverting everything since. Nothing warns you; the diff looks ordinary until you read its size. **The early tell is the push output:** `* [new branch]` instead of `abc..def` means the remote branch is gone because the PR already merged — I had recreated it by accident and had to `git push origin --delete`. **Two rules.** (1) After any merge of my own PR, follow-up work starts with `git checkout -b <new> origin/main`, never on the merged branch. (2) Before opening any follow-up PR, run `git diff origin/main --stat` and confirm the file count matches what I actually edited — a diff far larger than the edit means a stale base, not a big change. A related trap in the same family: `git log origin/main..HEAD` listing a commit as unmerged is *normal* after a squash merge and does **not** mean work is unlanded — verify by content (`git diff origin/main HEAD --stat`, empty = fully landed) before deleting a worktree or branch, which is what made removing the #1166 worktree provably safe.

- [2026-08-05] **An isolation proof only covers the axis it measured; shared *per-user* host state sits outside a per-work-root boundary and fails as one job killing another (#1178).** #981 proved `cowrite-windows` and `cowrite-windows-2` use distinct `_work` roots, and #982 removed the global concurrency group on the strength of it. But sccache's server is a per-**user** daemon, and both services run as the same account — so the compiler cache was never inside the boundary that proof established. #978 had wired `RUSTC_WRAPPER=sccache` while the runners were still serialized, so the shared daemon was never exercised concurrently until it was. The failure mode is not a workspace collision, which is what everyone was watching for: in run `31042402771` the daemon was spawned by whichever job first touched sccache, and was reaped when *that* job completed, so ~30 in-flight crates in the *other* job died together with `os error 10054` 890 ms after the peer reached `Complete job`. Two independent tells made it diagnosable — the sub-second correlation with the peer's completion, and `Compile requests 0` from a `--show-stats` that had just followed ~120 compilations, proving the reporting client had spawned a *fresh* daemon rather than reaching the one that had been serving. The fix is ownership: a Task Scheduler task owns the daemon so no job's cleanup can reap it. **Generalizable: before letting two runners execute concurrently, enumerate the host state they share and ask of each "is this scoped per work-root or per user?" A per-user resource needs an owner outside any job, and until it has one, concurrency turns it into a mutual-kill channel. The same audit caught a quieter instance in the same tool — `sccache --zero-stats` was mutating shared state, silently wiping the counters a peer job was accumulating, which retroactively invalidates any overlapping-run hit rate recorded between #982 and #1178.**

- [2026-07-31] **Coalescing native watchdog observations to child exit extends the lifetime of the protection evidence they depend on (#990, D21/FR-AGENT-2/5/6).** A provider may announce a write before its truncate-and-replace writer closes, so converting on the stream event or native notification can turn the transient empty file into a deletion-only proposal. The terminal child-exit scan is the first sound read edge, but deferral means a tab-only document that closes mid-run must retain its watchdog identity and original baseline while its announcement is outstanding; otherwise tab close removes it from the scan and launders the raw overwrite as the next open's baseline. The retained entry is released only after terminal reconciliation consumes or drops the announcement and the run ends. This deliberately widens the interval in which raw agent bytes remain on disk to the active run's lifetime; a force-quit in that interval can leave the overwrite unconverted, an accepted risk of #990's coalescing direction rather than an unnoticed guarantee.

- [2026-07-27] **#497 correction: D21 run targets and provider write paths are two distinct boundary facts, and both must be captured at submission/observation time.** CJ ratified the default operation-target policy routed out of TD-17: the active document at request submission is pinned for the queued/active run's whole lifetime; other open tabs remain protected only through the independent live-tab source; a repo-level request with no active document carries no target. The first production-wiring review then executed Claude Code 2.1.220 and found its `Write.file_path` is a canonical absolute path (including macOS `/private/var` spelling), while the watchdog keys tabs, targets, native notifications, and sidecar identity by canonical project-relative strings. The run observer now confines every provider-announced path to the canonical open-project root and converts it through `identity_relative` before either ledger attribution or eager inspection. The same accepted-run set gates `observe` and `end_run`, so a D23/provider request rejected for another project cannot inject or flush attribution in the open project's watchdog. The shipped fake-agent fixture now expands `${PROJECT_ROOT}` to its canonical process cwd and emits that absolute shape; without normalization the real spawned-run conversion test goes red.
- [2026-07-27] **A filesystem watcher for a dynamic protected set should keep one stable OS subscription and filter against live membership, while the run stream closes notification-order races (#497, D21/TD-17).** Re-subscribing individual tab/target paths on every open, close, or run transition creates gaps exactly where D21 says protection must survive tab close. `OpenProject` therefore owns one recursive native subscription for its lifetime; each event consults the watchdog's shared protected union before reading the file. Native delivery and child stdout are independent streams, so neither order is sufficient alone: the run observer records each raw `Write`/`Edit`/`MultiEdit` event and immediately inspects its targets (covering write-before-stream-drain), the native watcher handles stream-before-write, and the child-exit edge performs one final scan before discarding unmatched announcements. Real Claude Code reports canonical absolute tool paths, so provider announcements are confined and normalized to the same project-relative filesystem identity as tab/run/native-watch producers before they can become attribution evidence; events from a run that never began against this open project are ignored. Native backends may emit several events for one write, so an unattributed change advances the watchdog's disk baseline after it surfaces once; duplicate events become `Unchanged` instead of repeatedly surfacing the same edit or letting a later agent event claim it. **CJ ratification (2026-07-27):** the default run operation target is the active document captured at submit; that pin survives tab close through the run, other tabs are protected only by their independent live-tab membership, and a repo-level request with no active document has no target. The durable rule is to keep ownership/lifecycle at the TD-17 aggregate, correlation at the queue that owns stable run ids and raw events, and dynamic membership in one shared union—not in the OS subscription list.
- [2026-07-27] **`TcpStream::try_clone` is not free on Windows: the duplicated handle is briefly inheritable, so a concurrent `CreateProcess` can leak a live connection into a child and turn the eventual close abortive.** The self-hosted Windows runner had been failing `agent_run_mcp_injection` at `common::mcp_call`, ~20% of runs, on whichever test happened to race — reported as `ConnectionReset` (WSAECONNRESET 10054) reading the response. Instrumentation ruled out the obvious suspects: the server had parsed the full request (96-byte body) and written a complete 401, and the client had received all 176 bytes — only the EOF was missing. An isolated repro pinned it exactly: with `BufReader::new(stream.try_clone()?)` and a thread spawning `cmd /c exit` in a loop, 1–3 resets per 1000 requests; with `BufReader::new(&stream)` and the same spawning, 0 per 4000; with no spawning at all (idle *or* CPU-saturated), 0 either way — so it is process *creation*, not load. Mechanism: Rust's `Command` always calls `CreateProcess` with `bInheritHandles: TRUE`, and a spawn racing the window where the duplicate is still inheritable leaves the child holding a reference — the server's `closesocket` is then not the last one, no `FIN` goes out, and when the child exits the kernel tears the socket down with an RST. Cowrite hits this for real because the same process spawns agent CLIs while the MCP server is serving. Fixed by borrowing the stream in all three hand-rolled HTTP endpoints (`agent::mcp`, `crates/test-driver`, `crates/fake-agent`); `impl Read for &TcpStream` exists precisely so the duplicate is unnecessary. CJ chose a static workspace-wide guard (`no_socket_is_read_through_a_duplicated_handle`) over a behavioural stress test, since the defect is probabilistic and a 3000-iteration test would tax every run to catch it ~99% of the time. **Generalizable: a low-single-digit-per-mille failure that only appears under concurrent process spawning is a handle-inheritance race, not "the runner is flaky" — and a duplicated OS handle you did not need is the thing to look for first. When a defect is probabilistic and platform-specific, a deterministic static guard on the banned construct is a better permanent test than a stress test tuned to fail often enough.**
- [2026-07-26] **A self-hosted Windows runner service can see a Microsoft Store `pwsh.exe` alias and still be forbidden to execute it — selecting a shell by name is part of the service contract, not merely a tool prerequisite.** #709's post-rebase run `30229510868` reached checkout and Rust setup, then failed twice before Cargo could start: Actions resolved its default run shell to `C:\Users\williacj\AppData\Local\Microsoft\WindowsApps\pwsh.EXE`, and the service received `Access is denied`. Pinning inbox Windows PowerShell 5.1 exposed the next service-policy layer in run `30229736626`: Actions could launch it, but the host disabled the generated temporary `.ps1`. The workflow therefore uses a custom 5.1 shell template with a process-scoped `-ExecutionPolicy Bypass` (no machine-policy mutation); a workflow-shape test prevents either failure mode from silently returning. **Generalizable: PATH resolution that succeeds interactively is not execution proof for a service account — pin an inbox/native executable when an app-execution alias can win the lookup, and remember that Actions runs commands through a generated script still subject to the service's execution policy.**
- [2026-07-26] **Review round 3 on #687 closed the remaining fixture-only evidence gap with the real Windows runtime.** The detector's dependence on `powercfg /a` wording had been pinned only by hand-written fixtures, in the same file where #278 and #342 showed why fixture-only evidence can miss a runtime failure. A Windows-only test now runs the real command, asserts the production shape guard, and cross-checks the strict parser against a looser independent `(S0` signal over the same available section. It passed on the self-hosted runner in run `30218374097` as subtest `ok 1004`. **Generalizable: when a fixture-pinned parser reads output from a runtime unavailable locally, compare the strict parser with an independent loose signal over the real input; disagreement exposes the silent-false-negative branch fixtures cannot reach.**

- [2026-07-26] **#708: checking `RunQueue::state()` before replacing an `OpenProject` would still tear the live run's boundary down — the idle check and project transition have to share the queue's own lock.** `start_run` installs the active run while holding `RunQueue`'s inner mutex, then reads the project's bound server in the same queue→project lock order. The safe inverse command is therefore not “read state, then swap”: `RunQueue::run_if_idle` holds that inner mutex through the whole open/close transition, so no start can land in the check→teardown gap and the lock order stays consistent. The command returns a typed `RunActive` error and leaves the server/token/watchdog untouched. One cross-layer consequence mattered just as much: `App.openProjectRoot` deliberately degrades through an MCP `Bind` failure and opens the shell anyway, but doing that for `RunActive` would show project B while the backend and live protected run remain on A. The frontend must distinguish those two tagged errors: bind failure degrades; active-run refusal keeps the shell on the old project. **General rule:** when a backend rejection exists to preserve authoritative state, every optimistic caller must treat it as a veto, not fold it into a generic “degrade and continue” path.

- [2026-07-23] **A fault-injection seam stays honest when it can change the world but cannot fabricate a failure (#538).** test-plan §4 names "mid-batch interrupt" as a required J4 acceptance highlight, but a *partial* `address_all_with_ai` was unreachable from outside the backend: every target in one batch appends to the same per-author sidecar, opened and closed fresh per thread, so making that file unwritable up front fails the **first** write ("none complete") rather than "some complete, then fail" — and racing a real filesystem mutation into the gap between two appends needs a sleep, which §7 forbids. The two obvious escapes were both wrong: injecting a writer double is mocking in e2e (CLAUDE.md forbids), and the in-process closure `run_batch_address` already takes is reachable only from that module's own unit tests. **The shape that worked:** `address_all_with_ai_watching(..., after_append: impl FnMut(usize, &Path))` — a window called after each *landed* append, told how many have landed and **which file the batch is writing to**, returning `()`. The `-> ()` is the load-bearing part of the design: the window can make that real file read-only, but it has no channel to hand back an error, so every failure the batch reports still comes out of the real `store::service` write. Policy (chmod) lives in the D23 driver (`crates/test-driver`, never shipped) behind a one-shot `/arm_batch_address_fault`; production passes a no-op closure and keeps no test-only branch. Consuming the arming with the batch it arms — and restoring permissions when that batch returns — is what makes the *retry* half of the journey testable in the same script, which is where the real UI regression risk lives (does the header still say "1 addressed · 2 untouched"?). **Generalizable:** when e2e needs a failure the system can't be talked into producing, don't fake the failure — find the narrowest place to hand the test a window on the real world, and make the seam structurally incapable of inventing an outcome. Cross-crate duplication of the tiny `make_unwritable` helper is deliberate: `cowrite_lib` cannot depend on the driver that depends on it, and moving "make a file read-only" into production code to dedupe a test helper would be the worse trade.

- [2026-07-21] **Two independent coordinate axes cross the CM6↔backend boundary, and only one of them was ever guarded (#447).** The codebase handles the byte↔UTF-16 axis carefully and visibly (`textops::Utf16Map`), which made it easy to assume offsets were a solved problem. They weren't: CM6's `Text` counts **every line break as exactly one position** whatever its literal bytes, while `serializeDocumentState` rejoins lines with the document's real `lineBreak` — two UTF-16 units on CRLF. So on a CRLF file, CM6 offsets and indices into the serialized string diverge by one unit per preceding line break, and *nothing typed or tested caught it* (both sides are `number`, both are honestly "UTF-16"). `EditorShell` was parsing the CRLF text and feeding the returned offsets straight to CM6 as decoration positions **and** as the ranges `decisionsTransaction` replaces on accept/reject — so accepting a change on a CRLF document edited the wrong range of the source of truth (TD-2, P1). Not cosmetic. **The generalizable lesson:** an offset is meaningless without the exact string it indexes, so any API taking `(text, offset)` has a coordinate-space precondition that must be stated at the boundary and satisfied by a *named* projection, not by whichever string happened to be in scope at the call site. The fix is `document.ts`'s `editorOffsetText(state)` — deliberately a coordinate projection, never a normalization (`serializeDocumentState` remains the only thing that goes to disk). **Sequencing worth noting:** PR #446 fixed the *opposite* direction of this same axis (CM6→disk, `handleQuote`) with a good inline comment, and the reviewer correctly filed the disk→CM6 half rather than letting it evaporate — but the two halves were one mechanism, and fixing one direction with a local comment left the other live for a full card. When a fix's rationale is worth six lines of comment, that is usually the signal it wants to be a named helper the *next* call site is forced to reuse.

- [2026-07-21] **SUPERSEDED by TD-17 (ratified by CJ 2026-07-22, PR #599), which answered this question differently — the shape described below was never built; see the [2026-07-26] #472 entry above for what landed and why. Kept because the reasoning about the *absence* of a lifecycle hook is exactly what TD-17 went on to name as the root.** **#472 "no production caller for `ProjectMcpServer`" — the fix was a *design* call about where a binding lives, and the honest answer was "first run against a root stands in for project open".** #344's three-way split left the injection seam unowned: #412 was explicitly forbidden from binding a server, #413 handed the wiring to #412 (already merged), #414 assumed a bound server existed. The blocker both #412 and #413 hit is real and still real: **there is no `open_project` Tauri command at all** — a project "opening" is purely client-side folder-picker state in `App.tsx`, so "bind on project open" has no lifecycle hook to attach to. The two candidate shapes were per-project (needs that missing hook) and per-run (bind inside the spawner off `RunRequest::project_root`). **Chose per-project, with the first run against a root standing in for the open event** (`agent::mcp::ProjectMcpBinding`, a `Mutex<Option<(PathBuf, ProjectMcpServer)>>` the spawner owns): `bind_to` binds once per root and hands every later run the *same* address and token, and a `bind_to` against a *different* root is the project change — the previous server is dropped (accept loop stopped, socket closed) before the call returns. **Rejected per-run binding** because it satisfies TD-1's injection but contradicts both #413's on-project-open semantics ("the token does not outlive the session it was minted for" degenerates to "does not outlive the turn") and M7's "one MCP server per project", and would churn a port, a thread and a credential per prompt. Forward-compatible by construction: when an `open_project` command lands it calls `bind_to` eagerly and nothing else changes. Two consequential API choices fell out: `commands::agent::claude_invocation` now takes the bound server **by value, not `Option`**, so "a run with no Cowrite tool access" stops being a representable state; and a bind failure maps to `SessionError::Spawn` (the run's `Failed` phase) rather than degrading to a tool-less spawn — a run that cannot deliver tracked changes is a failed run, not a quietly degraded one (D21). One wiring gotcha: `RunQueue`'s `Spawn` was being constructed with a bare `fn` pointer in both `lib.rs` and `crates/test-driver`, which cannot carry the binding — both had to become closures capturing an `Arc<ProjectMcpBinding>` (the `Spawn` trait already had the blanket `Fn` impl, so no trait change was needed).

- [2026-07-21] **Proving "the injection actually works" needed the fake agent to speak *real* loopback HTTP — the in-process `ToolServer` stub cannot distinguish a working wire from a broken one.** Every existing fake-agent MCP test dispatches through `StubToolServer`, which answers canned text regardless of what `--mcp-config` said — so an end-to-end assertion built on it would pass just as happily with the injection deleted. Added `fake_agent::HttpToolServer` (+ a `--mcp-config <json>` flag on the binary, inline JSON exactly as the real `claude` CLI takes it): it parses the injected config's `url`/`Authorization`, and dispatches each `mcp_call` step as a real JSON-RPC `tools/call` over loopback. Hand-rolled HTTP, zero new dependencies — the same precedent `cowrite_lib::agent::mcp` and `crates/test-driver` already set. The stub stays the default for every playback scenario (deterministic, no network); the HTTP server only engages when a config is actually injected, and a *malformed* config is a hard exit rather than a silent stub fallback, since a silent fallback is precisely the failure mode that would make a broken injection look green. **Test-design note: pick an assertion only the real server can satisfy.** The e2e scenario asserts `get_document_state` returns the project's actual file content at its actual `textops::Document::revision()` — the stub answers `"# Stub document…"`, so a fall-back fails. Verified by mutation twice: reverting `mcp_server: Some(..)` to `None` fails 3 tests, and forcing the binary back onto the stub fails both e2e tests. **Also extracted `src-tauri/tests/common/mod.rs`** — `fake_agent_bin`/`workdir`/`fake_agent_scenario` had been copy-pasted into four integration suites (one of which even commented "no shared integration-test support crate here"); this card would have made it five.

- [2026-07-20] **This dev machine intermittently blocks a freshly-rebuilt `fake-agent.exe` with "An Application Control policy has blocked this file" (os error 4551) — environmental, not a product defect.** Hit while re-running `cargo test --workspace` during the #412/PR #452 review-loop pass: several `tests/agent_run_queue.rs` cases failed with that OS error from `AgentSession::spawn`, spuriously, and reran clean without any code change. Confirmed pre-existing by `git stash`-ing every review-loop edit and reproducing the identical failure on the unmodified `ab524d7` tree — same class as the gh-stub flake noted in the 2026-07-19 entry below, different binary. Deleting `target/debug/fake-agent.exe` and letting `cargo build` recreate it cleared the block for that run; two subsequent full `cargo test --workspace` runs came back clean. Whatever's scanning/gating newly-written executables on this machine appears to race a test binary that spawns one within the same process launch — not something to fix in this repo; noted so a future engineer hitting the same OS error on this box doesn't chase it as a regression.

- [2026-07-20] **#412/PR #452 review addendum — "the terminal snapshot races past" was the wrong diagnosis; it is never externally visible at all, and the fix is a value, not a wait.** The Reviewer's Important 1 corrected the test-design note below: `drain_and_advance` finalizes a run's terminal `RunPhase` and (if anything is pending) starts the next run's `spawn_and_track` inside the SAME `Mutex<Inner>` lock acquisition — so `active` never passes through an externally-observable terminal state for a run with a queued successor, not merely "usually doesn't" or "a poller has to get lucky." No amount of polling frequency fixes that; it's a value that a single-slot `active` field structurally cannot hold two things in. Fix: `Inner`/`RunQueueState` gained `last_finished: Option<RunStatus>`, written in `drain_and_advance` right alongside `active`'s own phase update (same lock, before the possible advance), so a poller that only ever sees `active` skip from run A to run B can still read A's outcome off `last_finished`. Also closed Important 2 (same review): the command layer (`commands::agent::start_run`'s empty-prompt rejection, `spawn_claude_run`'s argv/cwd composition) was reachable by no test — every `RunQueue` integration test injects `spawn_scenario`, so the real spawner was the one thing never run. Split two plain functions out of the `#[tauri::command]`s (`validate_prompt`, `claude_invocation`) purely so they're callable without a Tauri `State`; each is now pinned by a direct unit test (cwd = project root, prompt reaches `-p`, `BINARY` is what's named, `mcp_server` stays `None`) — the composition itself, not just `build_args` in isolation, is what a regression there would have silently broken. Both suites green after; no frontend files touched.

- [2026-07-20] **`commands::agent` run queue (#412, split of #344) — killing a session being drained on another thread needs the kill split out of the session, not a `Mutex<AgentSession>`.** FR-AGENT-7's queue drains an active `AgentSession`'s stream on a background thread (`next_event()` in a loop) while `stop` runs on the IPC-command thread; a plain `Mutex<AgentSession>` deadlocks that pairing — the reader would hold the lock for the whole blocking `read_line`, and the killer could never acquire it to call `kill()`. Fix: `AgentSession`'s `child: GroupChild` moved behind `Arc<Mutex<GroupChild>>` (stdout stays outside any lock — only the reader ever touches it, by construction), plus a cheap cloneable `KillHandle` (`kill_handle()`) that can kill the group from any thread without touching `stdout` at all. Proven with a test that would itself hang on a regression (`kill_handle_stops_a_hung_session_being_drained_on_another_thread`, against `claude-never-terminates.json`) rather than asserting the property indirectly. **`RunQueue` is a single global instance, not keyed per project** — tech-design §3.5 finding M7 (one project per app instance; opening an already-open project focuses the existing window) already makes FR-AGENT-7 "enforceable by construction" at the process level, so per-project keying would be unused generality. **Scope boundary respected literally:** `agent::claude::SpawnRequest::mcp_server` went from `McpServer` to `Option<McpServer>` so `commands::agent::spawn_claude_run` can build a real, valid headless `claude` invocation *without* a bound MCP server — #412's card explicitly forbids binding one (that's #413); a run started today streams and can be stopped, just has no Cowrite tool access until #413 lands and swaps `None` for a real bound server. Test design note: queue-ordering tests that stop an active run *and* have a queued run behind it must not poll the stopped run's own id for its terminal phase — the moment it resolves, the same locked step immediately overwrites the active slot with the auto-advanced next run, so a poll can race past the terminal snapshot entirely (`tests/agent_run_queue.rs`'s `wait_until_active` polls on identity for exactly this reason; `wait_until_terminal` is only safe to use when nothing is queued behind the run being watched).

- [2026-07-20] **#413 "bind the MCP server on project open" (split of #344) — added the lifecycle wrapper, deliberately left the Tauri command wiring for the sibling.** #344 (escalated, no-progress) tried to do "agent run commands + MCP server startup" as one card; splitting it meant deciding where the boundary actually falls, since no `open_project` Tauri command exists yet at all (the frontend's `App.tsx` never calls into the backend beyond `open_document`/`load_tab_session` — a project "opening" is purely client-side folder-picker state today). Followed `store::lock::ProjectLockGuard`'s own precedent exactly: that module's doc comment already says "leaves calling `WebviewWindow::set_focus` to whichever `commands/` layer wires up project-open" — i.e. the lock primitive shipped (#152/P2a-11) before any command used it. Built `agent::mcp::ProjectMcpServer` the same way: `open(root)` binds a `LoopbackMcpServer` (P2a-12, unchanged) and serves it on a background thread; `mcp_server()` hands back `agent::claude::McpServer` (P2a-15, unchanged) for the sibling run-commands card to feed into `SpawnRequest`; `Drop` stops serving and closes the socket — "closing the project unbinds it and the token does not outlive it" tested by asserting a fresh connect to the old address is refused after drop. The one new mechanism: `LoopbackMcpServer::serve_until` (nonblocking `accept()` + a stop-flag poll, 10ms) sits *alongside* the existing blocking `serve` rather than replacing it — collapsing them would have added a poll-interval latency to every request in the already-reviewed `tests/mcp_server.rs` suite for a caller (this one) that didn't need it. `Debug` is hand-written to redact the token (`"<redacted>"`) as a belt-and-braces token-leakage-sweep case, since there's no structured-logging facility yet to run the test-plan §3 automated log scan against. Verified: `cargo fmt`/`clippy -D warnings` scoped to the touched files only — a stray `cargo fmt` (no `-p`) reformatted several unrelated pre-existing-drift files across the workspace (rustfmt/toolchain version skew from CI's pinned `stable @ 2026-07-09`) and had to be reverted; `cargo fmt -- <file>` still reformats the whole workspace, not just the named file — use `rustfmt --check <file>` directly to scope a check.

- [2026-07-19] **Issue #237 ("P2a-5 redline toolbar count stuck at 0/0 — async seed effect doesn't trigger a React re-render") is already fixed on `main` — no code change needed.** #237 was filed 2026-07-18 at 12:47Z off a real reproduction against `a0cfbf3` (see the 2026-07-18 Deferred Fixes entry below); the identical race was independently found and fixed the same afternoon while e2e-verifying #157/P2a-8 — landed in PR #242, opened 14:18Z and merged 17:09Z, roughly 4h22m *after* #237 was filed, so the two never crossed paths in the queue — by the time #237's card reached an engineer the fix had already been sitting on `main` for roughly 21 hours, not "a day." Confirmed by inspection and `git blame`: `EditorShell.tsx`'s `updateListener` (currently ~lines 292-310) already bumps `redlineVersion` on `update.docChanged || update.selectionSet || update.transactions.some(redlineChromeEffectsChanged)` — exactly the fix this issue's "Suspected cause / fix direction" section proposes (checking for `seedTrackedChanges`/`dropTrackedChanges` effects), generalized via a shared `redlineChromeEffectsChanged` combinator (`trackedChanges.ts`) also used by the decoration rebuild so the two "which effects matter" lists can't drift apart. `EditorShell.test.tsx` already carries the regression test #237 calls for (`"editor/EditorShell — the async redline seed refreshes the toolbar on its own (regression)"` — seeds via a manually-resolved promise *after* first paint, asserting `"0 / 0"` then `"1 / 1"` with no other doc/selection trigger), and `docs/test-matrix.md`'s `NI-redline-async-seed-refresh` row is already `present`, both traced to #242. No production or test diff opens against this card. **Baseline note, unrelated to this finding:** `cargo test --workspace` intermittently shows 1-2 failures in `store::watch::live_watch_tests` (FSEvents historical-event replay racing the fresh `TempDir`) — this is the pre-existing, already-filed **#284** flake (see the 2026-07-19 entry below); reruns pass, and `store/watch.rs` is untouched by (indeed, entirely unrelated to) this card. `vitest run`: 401 passed across 42 files, clean. Recommended closing #237 as a duplicate/already-fixed-by #242 in a comment on the issue — this docs-only journal entry is the PR's only diff.
- [2026-07-18] **`textops::deliver::validate` was checking TD-11 round-trip per marker *in isolation*, so a marker whose delivered position lands inside a `parse` exclusion zone was called representable yet silently vanished (#250, proptest-found via `prop_delivered_subset_is_never_corrupt`).** The predicate's per-edit pass calls `round_trips_alone`, which parses `parse(&marker)` on the marker's own bytes with no surrounding context; the whole-set pass only checked reveal-on-accept. Neither saw that once the marker is spliced into `live`, the *document's* structure can swallow it. The proptest-minimised case: `live = "    aAaaA0a"` (4 leading spaces) with three delimiter-fragment insertions — edits 0/1 accept to a live `{-- --}` (correctly refused by the reveal pass), but the surviving edit 2's marker is delivered onto the still-4-space-indented line, making the delivered line a CommonMark **indented code block** (an exclusion zone `parse` reads *no* markup from, #75/`exclusion_zones`), so the tracked change would be delivered-but-untracked — the exact TD-11 corruption the property guards. Key realisation: this is not about delimiters at all (the marker `{++...++}` is well-formed); it is purely about *where in the document structure* the marker lands, which only the whole document reveals. **Fix (right altitude — generalise, don't special-case indentation):** `validate` now runs a whole-document round-trip pass — render the currently-representable subset, `parse` it, and refuse any surviving marker the parser does not recover at its exact span *and* kind — folded into one joint fixpoint loop with the reveal-on-accept pass (refusal is monotonic, so it terminates in ≤ n rounds; over-refusal is safe, corrupt delivery is not). This catches *any* exclusion-zone/context truncation (fenced, frontmatter, inline-code), not just indented code. Attribution is `ExistingText`: a marker that round-trips alone but dies in context is defeated by existing document structure the agent does not own and cannot reword — an honest hard-fail, never a silent drop. **Shared latent bug:** `prop_clean_batch_always_delivers_and_round_trips` fails on the *same* `live = "     0"` input on `main` (verified by stashing the fix) — its "clean ⇒ always delivers whole" claim is false when `live` is indented, because a clean marker is swallowed too. That property isolates the *delimiter*-cleanliness dimension, so its `live` is now scoped to empty-or-non-space-leading (`([a-zA-Z0-9][a-zA-Z0-9 ]{0,23})?`) — the only exclusion zone reachable from `[a-zA-Z0-9 ]` is leading-indent code (no backticks/`---`/newlines), and with 0 leading spaces the delivered line's indent is always 0 (markers begin with `{`), so the strong claim holds; the indented case is covered instead by `prop_delivered_subset_is_never_corrupt` (full `live`, safety-only) plus two new unit regressions. Regression seed committed to the (already-tracked, by repo convention) `proptest-regressions/deliver.txt`.
- [2026-07-17] **Claude Code adapter session lifecycle (P2a-15, #148) — `CARGO_BIN_EXE_<name>` is same-package-only, so a cross-crate integration test has to build+locate the fake-agent binary itself.** `agent::claude` (pure `-p --output-format stream-json --verbose --mcp-config <inline JSON> [--resume <id>]` argv construction + the `McpServerConfig`-schema-valid inline config + a `claude_code_version` vs. `MIN_SUPPORTED_VERSION` drift check, TD §3.4) is kept separate from `agent::session::AgentSession` (spawn-as-process-group via `command-group`, line-by-line stream-JSON parsing into the J1 activity feed, `session_id`/version capture off `system/init`, `stop` = whole-group kill). The split matters for testability: `build_args` is a pure unit test, but proving spawn/stream/stop against a *real* subprocess needed the `fake-agent` binary (P0-6/P0-7/P0-8) standing in for `claude` — every existing fake-agent-spawning test lives *inside* the fake-agent crate itself and resolves the binary via `env!("CARGO_BIN_EXE_fake-agent")`. That macro is a compile-time constant Cargo only populates **for a binary target of the same package being built** — confirmed by writing a one-line probe test in `cowrite`'s own `tests/`: `error: environment variable CARGO_BIN_EXE_fake-agent not defined at compile time`, even with `fake-agent` added as a `[dev-dependencies]` path dependency (a lib-only link, which doesn't force the sibling crate's `[[bin]]` to build or its env var to populate). Fix: a small `OnceLock`-memoized test helper (`tests/agent_session.rs`) that runs `cargo build -p fake-agent --bin fake-agent` itself, then locates the produced binary by walking up from `std::env::current_exe()` (`.../target/debug/deps/<this-test-binary>` → `.../target/debug/`) — every workspace member's binaries land in the same shared `target/` dir regardless of which package's `Cargo.toml` requested the build, so this works cross-package where `CARGO_BIN_EXE_*` can't. **Considered and rejected:** mirroring `e2e/support/fakeAgent.mjs`'s established `cargo run -p fake-agent -- …` wrapper convention — appropriate for the JS/e2e side's build-on-demand convenience, but wrong here: `cargo run` interposes a `cargo` process between the test and the real binary, and this specific test's whole point is proving `command-group`'s process-group kill actually reaches the real child — production spawns `claude` directly, so the test should too, not through an extra indirection layer that risks masking a real group-membership gap. Stop-safety (FR-AGENT-6) is proven with a scenario that streams one turn then hangs *before* any `propose_changes`/write step is reachable — `stop()` must reap it via the group kill (a plain `Child::kill` on an already-hung leaf would still work, so the real teeth is that a *hung* process, not a `--never-terminates`-style already-exited one, still yields to the group kill) and the target doc's bytes are asserted unchanged both before and after. Full Rust workspace green (`cowrite_lib`'s 286 lib tests include 12 new `agent::{claude,session}` unit tests; `tests/agent_session.rs` adds 7 new integration tests against the real fake-agent subprocess), `docs/test-matrix.md` gets one new `NI-claude-adapter-session` row plus `FR-AGENT-1`/`FR-AGENT-6`/`FR-X-3` moved from `pending` to `partial` (this card lands the spawn/stream/resume/stop mechanism; the FR-AGENT-7 queue and the crash-mid-run/permission paths remain later units' jobs, named in each row's Notes).

- [2026-07-17] **Windows: `LockFileEx` refuses an append-only file handle — any fs4 lock on an `OpenOptions::append(true)` file fails with `Access is denied` (os error 5).** Found when P2a-17's transcript store (merged via #220, presumably developed/reviewed on macOS where `flock` has no access-right requirement) failed all six `store::transcript::append_read_tests` on Windows. Root cause: Rust's append mode deliberately opens with only `FILE_APPEND_DATA` (for atomic appends), but `LockFileEx` requires the handle to carry `GENERIC_READ` or `GENERIC_WRITE`. Fix is one line: add `.read(true)` to the `OpenOptions` — read access satisfies the lock's requirement without weakening append-only write semantics. Pattern to watch for: `store::lock` was unaffected only because it opens its lock files with write access; any future "open append-only, then fs4-lock" code path will hit this again, and only on Windows — macOS-only CI/dev keeps it invisible until someone pulls on a Windows machine.

- [2026-07-16] **Autosave + atomic writes + LF normalization (P2a-7, #144) — one backend write chokepoint carries both durability guarantees; normalization is the one sanctioned exception to P1 fidelity.** FR-ED-2 splits cleanly across the boundary: the *mechanism* (atomic write, LF normalization) is backend, the *policy* (per-file opt-out, autosave cadence, CRLF trigger) is frontend. **(1) All three save triggers route through the single `save_document` command** (autosave = manual = Save As is required parity — acceptance line), so making that one command atomic + normalizing means the guarantee holds however the save fired; a frontend-only normalize could be bypassed by a future backend caller. Atomicity **reuses `store::atomic::write`** (the sidecars' write-temp+rename helper) rather than a second copy — widened its visibility to `pub(crate)` and promoted its docstring from "sidecars only" to "any single-writer whole-file rewrite incl. the source-of-truth doc." Crash-safety is genuinely testable without a real crash: stage a leftover `.tmp` as if the process died before the rename, assert the target still holds the old complete bytes (`an_interrupted_write_leaves_the_original_file_intact`). **(2) LF normalization lives in `textops::normalize` (pure, `Cow`-returning)** — the one deliberate divergence from P1 `save∘load ≡ identity`, so it's property-tested to prove it touches *only* `\r` (CR-free input returns borrowed & byte-identical; idempotent; no `\r` ever survives). `save_document` gains a `normalize_line_endings: bool`; the P2a-1 "CRLF preserved verbatim" test survives as the `false` (opt-out) path, and a new test covers the `true` path. **(3) The Rust param name shadows the imported fn** — `normalize_line_endings(text)` won't resolve when a `bool` param owns that name; call it `textops::normalize_line_endings(...)` fully-qualified. **(4) The autosave debounce is pure and clock-testable** (`editor/autosave.ts`, no CM6/React) so `vi.useFakeTimers` covers coalescing/flush/cancel with no sleeps (test-plan §7); `EditorShell` arms it on `docChanged`, flushes pending on unmount (so a doc-switch never drops the last edits — `save` captures content synchronously, surviving `view.destroy()`), and fires the once-per-open `hasCrlf` trigger the P2a-21 banner will hook. Interval is a hardcoded `DEFAULT_AUTOSAVE_INTERVAL_MS` stub — P2a-9 wires it to Settings (FR-ED-2's "configurable"). **(5) The D23 test-driver mirrors the command surface** — its `SaveArgs` gained the flag as `#[serde(rename_all = "camelCase")]` + `#[serde(default)]`-on, matching both Tauri's JS→Rust camelCase convention and `ipc.ts`'s `JSON.stringify` wire body, so `normalizeLineEndings` reaches the real backend command over HTTP unchanged (proven end-to-end: `save_document_normalizes_crlf_to_lf_through_http`). **Two FR-ED-2 matrix rows** (rust + vitest) because the check enforces one kind per row and the tests genuinely span both layers — duplicate IDs are allowed. **Design call not escalated:** backend-normalization + reuse-atomic + debounce-autosave are standard engineering choices within TD-2/FR-ED-2's explicit scope; none contradicts a recorded decision, so no CJ pre-approval needed (proactiveness: implementation task done completely). **Review fix (#144, Important):** reusing the sidecars' `atomic::write` behind the *autosave* surface reintroduced a real hazard the single-writer sidecars never had — the helper stages every write in a *fixed* `<name>.tmp`, but autosave makes overlapping same-path saves reachable (manual-save-over-autosave, a debounced save while the prior write still lands, unmount-flush mid-write), and `EditorShell.save` was fire-and-forget with no in-flight guard. Two concurrent `save_document` calls would collide on that shared temp → spurious `ENOENT` at best, a torn source-of-truth file at worst (the exact P0 this card exists to prevent).
