Fixed at the frontend boundary with a single-flight `createSaveRunner` (coalesce overlapping requests into one *trailing* write carrying the latest content — last-issued wins, which a unique-temp-name fix would NOT give), restoring `atomic::write`'s single-writer premise; both the helper's and the command's docs now state the contract explicitly. Chose frontend-serialize over unique-temp-names because it also fixes save *ordering* (a stale older snapshot can't win the rename race) and keeps the shared sidecar primitive untouched. Also (Minor) broadened the CRLF trigger `hasCrlf`→`hasNonLfLineEndings` (`includes("\r")`) to match the transform exactly (normalization changes a file iff it has any `\r`), so a lone-CR file is offered the "Don't convert" opt-out too rather than being silently changed.
- [2026-07-09] **P0-9 review fixes (PR #57, two Important findings).** (1) The `#[ignore]` matcher used a fixed `[\s\S]{0,80}` char window, so a realistic `#[ignore = "long reason"]` (or attributes/doc-comments between `#[ignore]` and `fn`) pushed the `fn` past the window and the test was reported *active* when it was actually ignored — a false green. Replaced with structural parsing: for each `fn <id>`, walk the contiguous block of attributes/doc-comments/blank lines directly above it and flag `#[ignore…]`. Char-window scanning of attributes is unsound; parse the block. (2) `extractRequiredIds` required a `(` right after the FR ID to match the `(MUST/SHOULD/COULD)` tag — which silently dropped `FR-ED-6: Cut from v1:` (no priority tag), so the gate enforced 54 FRs when the spec defines 55. Keyed the match on the ID alone (`- FR-AREA-n\b`). Lesson: don't couple a completeness scan to an *optional* neighbouring token — descoped/cut requirements still need a row.
- [2026-07-09] **`node --test <dir>` broke on Node 26 but works on Node 20.** `node --test scripts/` tried to *load* `scripts` as a CJS module (`MODULE_NOT_FOUND`) on the local Node 26; directory auto-discovery semantics changed. Fixed by naming the file explicitly (`node --test scripts/check-test-matrix.test.mjs`) — version- and OS-agnostic (CI is Node 20; matters because the frontend job runs on the Windows runner too). Chose the built-in `node:test` runner over vitest for this tooling test: zero new deps and it doesn't pre-empt the frontend's own vitest setup (P2a-2). One parser constraint to remember: `Test IDs` cells are comma-split, so a test title containing a literal comma can't be referenced as-is — rename the test or it won't match.
- [2026-07-09] **`cargo test` in `src-tauri/` tests only the root package, not the workspace.** `src-tauri/` is simultaneously the workspace root *and* the `cowrite` package (`.`), so a bare `cargo test` there scopes to `cowrite` alone — textops and provider-contract tests never run. `--workspace` is required to run all members. CI already gets this right (`cargo test --manifest-path src-tauri/Cargo.toml --workspace`), but the CLAUDE.md shorthand "`cargo test` in `src-tauri/`" silently under-tests locally; always add `--workspace`.
- [2026-07-09] **JSON Schema draft 2020-12 `if/then` for drift-tolerant discriminated unions.** To validate a tagged-union event stream where unknown tags must still pass, don't use `oneOf` (fails closed on unknowns). Instead: require the discriminator at top level, then `allOf` a list of `{ "if": { "required":["type"], "properties":{"type":{"const":X}} }, "then": {…} }` clauses. Unknown tags match no `if` → no constraints → valid; known tags get their fields checked. `additionalProperties` inside a `then` sees only that subschema's own `properties`, so per-tag strictness must live in a `$ref`'d `$defs` object that declares all its properties. The `jsonschema` crate (0.26) auto-detects the draft from `$schema`; `validator_for(&value)` + `iter_errors`/`is_valid` is the stable API surface.
- [2025-02-12] Tauri `Channel<TSend>` is the correct way to stream from Rust to frontend. Create `new Channel()` on JS side with `onmessage`, pass to `invoke()`. Rust side calls `channel.send(data)`. Types must implement `Serialize`.
- [2025-02-12] `eventsource-stream` crate wraps reqwest `bytes_stream()` into typed SSE events. Combine with `tokio::select!` and `CancellationToken` for cooperative cancellation.
- [2025-02-12] GitHub Models API is at `models.github.ai/inference/chat/completions` — OpenAI-compatible format, uses GitHub OAuth token with `models:read` scope.
- [2025-02-12] macOS Keychain prompts "allow/deny" when a recompiled binary accesses stored credentials. "Always Allow" prevents repeat prompts.
- [2025-02-12] GitHub OAuth device flow `slow_down` responses increase the required poll interval. Frontend must respect the backend's interval, not hardcode its own.
- [2026-07-06] **Windows dev-machine setup for the v2 scaffold (P0-1).** WebView2 was preinstalled; installed Rust via rustup (stable 1.96.1, `x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`) and VS 2022 Build Tools 17.14.35 (VCTools workload, `--includeRecommended`) for the MSVC linker. Rust resolves `link.exe` from any shell — no vcvars needed. A freshly-spawned shell doesn't inherit rustup's PATH edit until restarted, so prepend `%USERPROFILE%\.cargo\bin` when scripting cargo. Tauri resolved to 2.11.5; first backend build ~1m23s.
- [2026-07-06] **Smart App Control is ENFORCED on this machine** (`HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\CI\Policy\VerifiedAndReputablePolicyState = 1`). It blocks running bare unsigned exes — a hand-built `rustc` hello.exe in `%TEMP%` was blocked ("An Application Control policy has blocked this file") — **but the cargo path works**: `cargo build`/`run` execute build scripts and the compiled unsigned binary, and the Tauri app launches (window + WebView2 children). Watch item for M6 signing (TD §3.6): SAC judges by reputation, so an early unsigned/renamed release binary could be blocked unpredictably — verify signed installers on a SAC-enforced machine.
- [2026-07-06] This machine's npm blocks package install scripts by default (`npm warn allow-scripts`; esbuild's postinstall was skipped). Vite still builds because esbuild loads its binary from the `@esbuild/win32-x64` optional dependency, not the postinstall. If a future dependency genuinely needs its install script, approve it explicitly rather than disabling the guard.
- [2026-07-09] **CI scaffold (P0-2) — optional-tool gating without masking failures.** eslint/vitest aren't configured yet, but the naive guard `npm run test 2>/dev/null || echo "not configured"` also swallows a *real* failing suite once it exists (any non-zero exit → `|| echo` → job green). Use `npm run <script> --if-present` instead: npm exits 0 when the script is absent but propagates a genuine failure. Also: trigger CI on `push:[main]` + `pull_request:[main]` (not `push:[feature/**]`) so feature branches run once via their PR, not twice; a `concurrency` group with `cancel-in-progress` kills superseded runs. `test-cargo` deliberately skips the frontend build — `tauri_build` tolerates a missing `frontendDist`, keeping the Rust test job independent of the TS build. Benchmark job is a stub (P2b-10); per test-plan §6 the real one must run on a dedicated/consistent runner, never a shared GitHub runner.
- [2026-07-09] **CI hardening (P0-2 follow-up).** Added `ubuntu-latest` to the matrix (matches the intended macOS/Windows/Ubuntu spread); Linux needs `libwebkit2gtk-4.1-dev`/`libgtk-3-dev`/`libayatana-appindicator3-dev`/`librsvg2-dev` installed for Tauri's `-sys` crates to link during clippy/test, whereas macOS/Windows ship the WebView system libs. Added `Swatinem/rust-cache@v2` (workspace `src-tauri`) so the three Rust-compiling jobs don't rebuild deps from scratch each run. Moved eslint out of the Rust `lint` job into `test-npm` so a failed `npm ci` can't fail the Rust checks, and dropped the now-unused Node setup from `lint`. `rustfmt` is platform-independent, so it runs once (ubuntu) instead of on every matrix leg. Scoped `cancel-in-progress` to `pull_request` events only — pushes to `main` now run to completion so each mainline commit keeps its own CI signal. Left actions on version tags (not SHA pins): low-value churn at this stage and `dtolnay/rust-toolchain@stable` is intentionally a rolling channel.
- [2026-07-09] **CI hardening pt.2 — typecheck job, `clippy --all-targets`, SHA-pinned actions.** Wired the frontend into CI: without a `tsc`/`build` step, a "green" pipeline said nothing about whether the TS scaffold compiled (eslint/vitest are still absent → `--if-present` no-ops). Added a `typecheck` npm script (`tsc --noEmit`) and run it once on ubuntu (platform-independent, like rustfmt). Broadened clippy to `--all-targets` so test/bench code is linted too. **Reversed the earlier "leave actions on version tags" call** (CJ requested pinning): all third-party actions now pinned to commit SHAs with the human tag in a trailing comment. **Gotcha:** `dtolnay/rust-toolchain` reads the toolchain *channel from the ref name* (`@stable`), so once pinned to a SHA it can no longer infer the channel — you MUST add `with: toolchain: stable` to every use, or the step errors with "unable to determine toolchain." `Swatinem/rust-cache` only maintains a `v2` major tag (no semver on the pinned SHA).
- [2026-07-15] **Windows file locks are mandatory and per-HANDLE — never store readable metadata inside the locked file (P2a-11, PR #179 review Critical).** `fs4::FileExt::try_lock` (and std's `File::try_lock`) is `flock` on Unix but `LockFileEx(EXCLUSIVE, 0, !0, !0)` on Windows: a *mandatory* whole-file byte-range lock scoped to the one locking handle. While held, no other handle can read **or write** the file — including a second handle opened by the *same process* (MS-documented). So `fs::write(lock_path, metadata)` after a successful `try_lock` fails with `ERROR_LOCK_VIOLATION` on Windows, and every cross-process `fs::read` of a held lock file errors out. Unix's advisory `flock` (I/O unaffected) makes this invisible on macOS/Linux — tests stay green locally and only Windows CI catches it. Pattern: keep the locked file content-free and put anything that must stay readable/writable (owner metadata, signals) in separate *unlocked* sidecar files (`lock.owner`, `lock.focus-request`). Recorded in TD-12 together with the `.cowrite/.gitignore` (`lock*`) exclusion of these machine-local runtime files from TD-4's committed-by-default `.cowrite/`.

- [2026-07-16] **Structural markdown decoration providers (P2a-3, #153, TD-13) — two `@lezer/markdown` integration gotchas.** Built `editor/structuralDecorations.ts` (headings/emphasis/lists/blockquotes as CM6 range decorations over a real `@lezer/markdown` GFM parse, per TD-13's ratified split: structural markdown parsing lives in the frontend, `textops::critic` stays the CriticMarkup/canonical-coordinate authority). **Gotcha 1 — `@lezer/markdown`'s `MarkdownParser` is not an `LRParser`.** `LRLanguage.define({parser})` (the obvious CM6 pattern) fails to typecheck: `MarkdownParser extends Parser` (the generic `@lezer/common` base), not the `@lezer/lr` `LRParser` `LRLanguage` requires (it wraps an inner LR parser for block/inline mixing internally). The fix is the seam `@codemirror/lang-markdown` itself uses: construct the base `Language` class directly — `defineLanguageFacet()` for the data facet, attach it to the parser's top node via `languageDataProp.add(type => type.isTop ? data : undefined)` passed through `parser.configure([GFM, {props: […]}])`, then `new Language(data, configuredParser, [], "markdown")`. **Gotcha 2 — `EditorState.create` defaults the cursor to position 0**, which sits on whatever construct starts the fixture's first line; the (correctly-working) active-line-reveal logic then reveals exactly the thing the first decoration-snapshot assertion checked, producing failures that look like a decoration bug but are a test-fixture bug. Fixed by giving every fixture a trailing neutral line and parking the test cursor there explicitly rather than relying on the default selection. **Design notes for the next card on this file (P2a-4 reveal-at-cursor polish, P2a-5 accept/reject):** decorations are a `StateField` (not a `ViewPlugin`) specifically so they're testable via plain `EditorState.create`/`.update()` with no mounted `EditorView`/DOM — `syntaxTree`/`Decoration`/`StateField` are all DOM-independent; only `EditorView` needs jsdom. Hiding is `Decoration.replace({})` (atomic, no CSS dependency) rather than a mark + `display:none` class. The active-line reveal here is deliberately *line-granularity only* for every construct (headings/lists/blockquotes/emphasis alike) per the card's "minimal baseline" instruction — SPIKE-3's node-granularity reveal for inline emphasis is explicitly P2a-4's polish, not built here. Also deliberately deferred: viewport-scoped recomputation (SPIKE-3's perf pattern, needs a `ViewPlugin`'s `view.viewport`) — this `StateField` recomputes over the whole document every transaction; fine for the correctness scope of this card, flagged as a follow-up if profiling real documents shows it's needed.

- [2026-07-17] **Copilot CLI (1.0.69) ships no read-only auth-status subcommand — `claude auth status --json` has a real Claude Code counterpart but Copilot doesn't, and a headless prompt invocation both costs a request and can dispatch to the model (P2a-22, #158).** Live-checked against `--help`/`login --help`/`mcp --help` before writing `agent::providers`: Claude Code's `claude auth status --json` is fast, local, and JSON (`{loggedIn, email, ...}`) — an ideal window-focus-pollable probe. Copilot has no equivalent; probing live cost a real request (`copilot -p "..." --allow-all-tools --log-level debug` actually dispatched and hit "You have exceeded your monthly quota" even with no env token set, meaning it silently picked up *some* stored/derived credential this session — undocumented, not reproduced further). Rather than guess at that undocumented path, `agent::providers`'s Copilot auth probe is a documented conservative approximation: the CLI's own stated headless-auth env-var precedence (`COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN` > `GH_TOKEN` > `GITHUB_TOKEN`, from `copilot login --help`) — real, read-only, zero-cost, but it under-approximates a `copilot login`-stored (credential-store-backed) session. Documented in the module rustdoc, the PR, and `docs/test-matrix.md`'s FR-X-7 row rather than silently shipped as if verified (FR-X-5: never silently no-op). **Lesson:** when a spec assumes symmetric capability across two providers (TD §3.4's capability matrix already treats Claude/Copilot asymmetrically elsewhere — permission delegation, hooks), verify each provider's *actual* surface live before writing the detection code, and if one side is missing the ideal mechanism, land the honest approximation with the gap named rather than spending further live-probe budget reverse-engineering undocumented behavior mid-card (that's spike-shaped work, not engineer-card work) — flag a follow-up instead (see Deferred Fixes).

- [2026-07-18] **P2a-14: change-set card + triage entry points (#159) — the timeline card and `TimelinePanel`'s render-switch, being separately-extendable per P2a-16's own contract, drifted out of sync mid-flight.** `timeline/types.ts`'s `ChangeSetCard`/`createChangeSetCard`, `timeline/ChangeSetCard.tsx` (summary/count header, per-section ▸ groups, `[Accept all]`/`[Reject all]`/per-section accept-reject each firing one atomic `onDecide(ids, decision)` call per FR-AGENT-6, and `[Review 1-by-1]` driving a pure state-machine pass, `timeline/changeSetReview.ts`, through the identical `RedlinePopover` component P2a-5's click-any-redline path already renders) were already written and passing their own suites when this card picked up — but `TimelinePanel.tsx`'s `renderCard` switch (the seam P2a-16's module doc names as the "extend without modifying" contract) hadn't been given its `"change-set"` case, so `tsc --noEmit` failed on the `never`-exhaustiveness check the moment `ChangeSetCard` widened the `TimelineCard` union. Wired the missing switch case + `index.ts` export (TDD: added a red `renders a change-set card through the same render-switch as a chat card` test mirroring P2a-18's own comment-card precedent, then made it green) and added the component's own dedicated `ChangeSetCard.test.tsx` (10 cases: render shape, accept-all/reject-all/per-section atomicity, 1-by-1 progress/advance/exit-mid-pass, and a regression pinning that the card adds no document-level keydown handling of its own — P2a-5 alone owns those shortcuts). **Simplify pass:** the local `decided` state was a `Map<number, ChangeSetDecision>` but only ever `.has()`-checked (the decision value was never read back — `onDecide` already carries it to the caller); narrowed to a `Set<number>`. **Scope boundary, explicit:** these entry points call `onDecide`/`onFocusChange` callbacks a caller wires to the live CM6 editor — the session/event wiring that would actually *dispatch* a change-set card from a real `propose_changes` delivery doesn't exist yet (no producer in the app today), so there is no live-app route to exercise this card yet; verification is the jsdom component-test layer (real DOM events/React reconciliation, not the full running app) plus the existing suites, matching the issue's own Depends list (P2a-1/P2a-5/P2a-13 land the pieces this card's callbacks will invoke). Test-matrix: new `FR-ED-4` vitest row for this UI/bulk/group/per-section layer, distinct from the already-`present` textops core and P2a-8 display-mode/nav UI rows.
- [2026-07-18] **`scripts/check-test-matrix.mjs`'s `Test IDs` cell splits on every comma with no escaping — a cited vitest title cannot itself contain a comma.** Found writing the P2a-14 (#159) matrix row: several new test titles used natural-language commas (e.g. "builds a change-set card carrying its change-set id, summary, and changes"), and `matrix:check` reported each comma-delimited fragment as a separate, nonexistent test id. Confirmed by scanning the whole matrix (`parseMatrix` + a grep for `,` inside any existing row's Test IDs cell): **zero** existing rows cite a test title containing a comma — an established, unwritten convention, not something this card discovered as a novel constraint. Fixed by renaming the affected test titles to drop the internal commas (e.g. "... its change-set id and summary and changes") rather than teaching the script quote-aware CSV parsing — a five-line title edit is cheaper than widening a shared script's parsing contract for one row, and matches the codebase's existing practice. **Lesson:** a matrix-cited vitest/e2e test title must avoid literal commas; phrase multi-clause titles with "and"/em dash instead.
- [2026-07-20] **A seam that carries positions must carry the revision those positions were measured against, or its consumer physically cannot be correct (P2b-4a #352, review addendum).** `setCommentThreads` was first defined as `StateEffect.define<readonly CommentThread[]>()` — a list of anchors with offsets, and no way to say *which text those offsets describe*. The field's own comment asserted the missing premise ("a re-seed is authoritative and already expressed against `tr.newDoc`") without anything enforcing it. Nothing was broken at merge time because the extension is installed nowhere yet; the point is that the *wiring* card (#259) could not have made it safe without reopening this file, because the effect type had no revision slot. Thread loading is async, so a user typing between the backend's read and the effect's dispatch makes every offset one revision stale and the layer draws confidently-wrong marks — exactly the failure D17/P2 exist to prevent, in a file whose every other path (orphaned/empty/out-of-range) already drops rather than guesses. **Fix:** the seed carries `{threads, revision}` and lands only when that matches `documentRevisionField` — which the document layer declares on load/save and which nulls itself on any local edit (a boolean check on the keystroke path, never a re-hash). Unknown revision ⇒ reject, deliberately: a missing highlight is visible and cheap, a wrong one is neither, and it forces the wiring card to supply the fact. A rejected seed keeps the anchors already in the field (CM6 has mapped them honestly through the same edits), so the degradation is a stale *colour*, never a stale *position*. **Lesson (generalizable):** when reviewing or designing an interface that transports offsets/positions/versions across an async boundary, ask "can the receiver tell whether this is still true?" — if the answer depends on a comment rather than a field, the seam is underspecified, and it is far cheaper to widen the payload while the seam has zero consumers than to catch it in the card that wires it. Second finding, same review: this layer's docblock cited "J3 Phase A severity-coloured highlights" as its authority while actually encoding thread *state* (open/addressed-pending/reopened/resolved), silently occupying J3's severity channel with a different axis; the citation was wrong, not the code — corrected, with the severity channel explicitly left open for Phase C, and each state given an underline style so the states differ by more than hue.

- [2026-07-26] **A two-stage matcher whose second stage writes its whole result back over the first will silently erase the first stage's matches — and the failure looks like "stage one is broken" (#594, TD-16 ruling 3).** The `parse_changes` join runs exact (anchor offset + snapshot) then unique-snapshot, accumulating into one `matched: Vec<Option<usize>>`. Stage two collected per-marker claims into its own array and then wrote every element back — `matched[i] = claim` — so a marker stage one had matched, for which stage two by construction has *no* claim (its record was consumed), was overwritten with `None`. The symptom was maximally misleading: the undrifted tests failed while the *drifted* one passed, i.e. the cheap total stage looked broken and the fuzzy one looked fine, when in fact stage one was correct and stage two was clobbering it. Fixed by writing back only `Some` claims. **The lesson is about the shape, not the typo:** an accumulator shared across passes needs each later pass to write only where it has something to say; a blanket write-back is a silent `None`-fill of everything the earlier pass had already decided. Worth writing the tests first here — the ordering of which tests failed (exact red, fuzzy green) was the whole diagnosis, and I would not have had it from a manual smoke test of one document.

- [2026-07-26] **Two surfaces that key the same map must share the key function, not agree about it (#594).** `docs.json` is minted by `agent::state` (canonicalize the confined path, strip the resolved root, forward slashes) and, since TD-16, *looked up* by `commands::changes`'s parse join. Re-deriving those six lines in the second surface would have worked on every test and drifted the first time either side changed — and the failure mode is invisible: a lookup that disagrees with the mint by one character silently returns "no records", which the join is designed to treat as the honest unrecorded case, so a whole document quietly loses its durable identities with nothing red anywhere. Extracted as `path_confine::identity_relative` and delegated `agent::state::relative_to_root` to it. **Generalizable: when the consequence of two implementations disagreeing is a fail-*safe* rather than a crash, the pressure to keep them in sync is gone — which is exactly when sharing the code, rather than the intention, is worth the small refactor.**
- [2026-08-11] **A React child keyed by a path is reused when the *document* at that path changes, so evicting the layer's own path-keyed maps fixes nothing (#932, PR #1360 round 1 Critical).** `TabbedEditor` renders one `EditorShell` per tab, keyed by `tab.path`, and `EditorShell` reloads on a `[root, path]` dep change. An external `mv a.md b.md` with both open makes the pane list go `["b.md", "a.md"]` → `["b.md"]`: React matches the surviving key against the **displaced** document's mounted pane, whose `(root, path)` never changed — so no reload, no read of the carried buffer, and the displaced document's text sitting under the moved document's identity (a save then writes the wrong document). The first instinct — evict `editorHandles`/`generations`/`loadedPaths`/`restoredContent` for the destination — is necessary and completely insufficient: none of those maps is what React reconciles on. **The identity a framework reconciles on has to change when the identity it stands for changes**; here that meant a per-path mount counter in the pane key (`b.md#1`), deliberately kept in the layer that owns pane mounting rather than adding tab ids to the store and the persisted session wire. The generalizable check: when a key is a *name for something else* (a path, an id you look up), ask what happens the day two different somethings hold that name in sequence — and whether any test would notice. Ours did not, because the collision case was tested one layer down in the pure store (`rekeyTabPaths` "never leaves two tabs on one path"), where React does not exist.
- [2026-08-14] **A "realistic positive" test and a genuine mutation pin are different artifacts when the production code being tested always produces the evidence for BOTH of two possible (one correct, one buggy) lookups together (#1504, extending #1497's own coverage from `outbox/<stream>.jsonl` to the data-reader cache).** `writeSnapshot` drops two claims per call — one for the data file's bytes, one for the metadata file's — because both files change on every refresh. That made the obvious test for "the metadata-only form is attributed via its OWN claim" (seed once, refresh again with the SAME text so only `confirmedAt` moves) pass identically whether `attributeDataReader`'s snapshot-form branch correctly keyed its claim lookup on the metadata file's sha256 or WRONGLY keyed it on the sibling data file's — because the data file's claim exists too, unspent, from the very same call, regardless of which form actually changed on disk. Reasoned through it on paper first and predicted the swap mutation WOULD redden that cell (counting claim instances by hand); running the mutation instead showed it did not — the reasoning missed that `writeSnapshot`'s two claims are independent map entries with independent counts, not a shared budget, so an accidentally-correct-looking lookup against the wrong key still finds real, unconsumed supply. Fixed by adding a SECOND, deliberately narrower cell: both the pre-seed and the in-bracket refresh run under this file's OWN ambient `NODE_TEST_CONTEXT` (vetoing `writeSnapshot`'s own self-claims entirely), and the only claim in play is one planted BY HAND for the exact metadata bytes about to land — so a lookup keyed on the sibling data file's sha256 finds nothing and the cell goes correctly blocking under the swap. **The general shape: when a producer's own realistic behavior always emits redundant evidence, a positive test built from that realistic behavior proves "the code does something that fits," not "the code consults the field you think it does" — isolating a mutation pin means removing every source of the SAME evidence the buggy path could also reach for, not just supplying the correct one.** Confirmed by execution rather than trusted from the design, per the standing lesson two sibling cards in this same file already logged (#1497, #1440): the swap mutation was applied and run against both cells before either was accepted as the pin, and only the hand-planted, ambient-vetoed cell reddened.
- [2026-08-17] **A `vi.fn()` with no implementation is not a fake of an async seam — it is a fake of a synchronous function that returns `undefined`, and a free-running poller is what eventually calls it (#1690).** The required Windows gate's `vitest` step failed intermittently on branches with zero `src/` files, once reporting `1315/1315 passed` and still exiting 1. The escape was `TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'content')` at `EditorShell.tsx:1205` — `disk.content`, where `disk = await openDocument(root, path)`. In production `openDocument` is `Promise<DocumentPayload>` and cannot resolve `undefined`; in `EditorShell.test.tsx` it was a bare `vi.fn()`, and `enterMissingWithOlderRead` called `mockReset()` (wiping the describe's persistent default) and then queued exactly two `mockResolvedValueOnce` answers against a shell mounted with `externalChangePollMs={1}`. **The number of reads FR-ED-7's poll takes is not bounded by anything a test awaits** — `createExternalChangePoller` rearms after every settle and keeps going for as long as the tab is mounted — so a third read is always possible and only *timing* decides whether it lands before RTL's `cleanup()` unmounts. It got `undefined`, and `void check().finally(...)` turned the throw into an unhandled rejection surfacing after the owning test had already passed, `.finally` rearming the next tick regardless so it repeated every millisecond until unmount. **Four things worth keeping.** **(1) The load hypothesis was right but not reproducible by loading the machine — it was reproducible by naming the window.** Six repeats of the file under 24 CPU hogs at load average 32 stayed green, as did a full-suite run and 8 further file runs with the detector verified live; a 25 ms `setTimeout` inserted at exactly the derived window (after the held read settles, before cleanup) in the CI-named test produced 19 unhandled `TypeError`s at the exact reported line on the first try. **When a flake is a race you can derive from the code, emulate the stall at the derived point instead of trying to lose the race by luck.** **(2) The first detector was blind, because vitest suppresses `console` by default.** An instrumented mock logging every unqueued read printed nothing across a full suite run and six file runs — and that silence was worthless, because `--disableConsoleIntercept` was missing and the mock could not have printed anything either way. Every "no unqueued reads" measurement quoted above is from runs after that flag was added and the detector had been seen to fire. **A silent detector and a clean measurement look identical; prove the detector fires before trusting a negative from it.** **(3) The same seam taught this in #368 and it recurred from the other side.** #368's fix was "model the disk as state the save mutates" rather than queueing an answer sequence; #1690 is the same class one layer down — the queue does not run out in the wrong *order*, it runs out *at all*. The repair is at the fake's default rather than per-test: an unqueued read now returns a read that has not come back yet, which is a state the poller genuinely waits in and cannot be mistaken for disk bytes. A separate sibling in the same file asserted an exact read total against that same self-rearming poll. **A test that asserts an exact count against a self-rearming poller is asserting a fact about the machine, not about the code** — and this one did it three ways at once: it captured the total after merely "more than one read" (a condition the mount's own reads already satisfied), it asserted that total *after* releasing the held read, which rearms the poll onto an already-settled `mockReturnValue` so it spins a fresh read every millisecond into the assertion window, and its banner assertion raced those same reads, whose stale bytes would raise the very banner it asserts is absent. **(4) My first fix to that case was insufficient, and only a loaded A/B caught it.** Repairing the capture condition alone made it pass at every poll interval from 1 ms to 120 ms and pass 10/10 on a quiet machine — then it failed `expected 6 to be 4` once in 10 runs under load, which is what exposed the settle-rearm leg. Reasoning had convinced me the poller was parked; it was, right up until the test itself unparked it. Had I shipped after the interval sweep I would have moved this card's flake from one assertion to another and called it fixed. **A race fix is a claim to re-measure under load, not one to reason to.** The final shape asserts the count at the Missing exit, where the property actually lives, and parks the poll on a second held read before releasing the first. **The end-state measurement, both arms alternated under one sustained load with an identical 25 ms stall:** pre-fix **6/6** runs red with 19–21 unhandled `TypeError`s and every test still reporting passed, post-fix **0/6**. Unamplified the pre-fix file reproduced spontaneously **1 run in 32** at load average 75–97, vitest naming the same test CI named — which is also the honest limit of the unamplified arm: 0-of-N at a single-digit rate is weak evidence, and the amplified A/B is what carries the claim. No product code changed; FR-ED-7's behavior is untouched.

- [2026-08-20] **A mount effect can observe a sibling effect's synchronous ref mutation while still holding the mount render's stale state (#1393).** `e2e/acceptance.spec.mjs:661` failed 3-of-4 local runs restoring only one of two tabs after `page.reload()`. `TabbedEditor`'s request effect assigned `rootRef.current` before awaiting `loadTabSession`; the later `[tabs]` effect then ran its mandatory mount pass with that new root and the render's initial `EMPTY_TABS`, letting `saveTabSession(root, empty)` race and sometimes replace the file before the read returned. The safe boundary is narrower than “restore succeeded”: only session writes are unsafe while the read is pending. Gating the whole multipurpose effect on `readyRoot` also disabled `setOpenTabs` and owner notifications, permanently after a genuine read error, violating D21's protected-open-tab feed and regressing J2's Missing-on-disk journey. The final fix records the pending restore request in a ref, skips only `saveTabSession` during that interval, and clears the marker on both success and failure; a rejection cell proves a later tab still reaches both `setOpenTabs` and persistence. Separately, `saveTabSession` writes are serialized on `sessionSaveRef`, mirroring `setOpenTabs`'s `tabFeedRef`, so an earlier slow snapshot cannot overwrite a later one. General lesson: guard the hazardous side effect at its own boundary; a readiness guard around an effect with several responsibilities silently widens failure scope.

- [2026-09-04] **#2327: a capability the frontend depends on can be absent from the ACL for months while every suite stays green, because every suite mocks the boundary that would have refused it.** `closeWindow` has called `getCurrentWindow().destroy()` since P2a-6 (#209, `f441b19c1`); `capabilities/default.json` never granted `core:window:allow-destroy`, which `core:window:default` deliberately excludes — read out of the generated `acl-manifests.json` rather than assumed. Quitting was therefore impossible in every real build ever produced, while `TabbedEditor.test.tsx` drove the entire quit sequence through a dozen cells asserting `destroyWindowMock` was called, and the D23 e2e surface has `isTauri()` false and never reaches the window API at all. Neither substitution is wrong — jsdom has no native window. What was missing is a test on the *other* side of the seam, and the real one turned out to be cheap: `tauri::generate_context!()` resolves the actual capability files, and `Context::runtime_authority_mut().resolve_access("plugin:window|destroy", "main", "main", &Origin::Local)` asks exactly what the running app asks. Twenty lines, no new dependency, red at the pre-fix head reproducing the reported ACL denial verbatim. (`runtime_authority_mut` is `#[doc(hidden)]` and documented as unstable, so a Tauri upgrade may break the call — a compile error in a test, which is the visible failure direction.) **Generalisable: wherever a test substitutes a boundary, the grant that boundary enforces is unverified by construction — and that grant is normally declared in a file that is itself testable.** #933 (`dialog:allow-save`) was the same class, found the same way — by a human using the app — and shipped with no test either. **A second finding, worth keeping separate because it survives the fix:** the failure did not merely fail, it lied. `finalizeCloseAttempt` wrapped the session write and the window destroy in one `try`, so a *successful* commit followed by a refused destroy raised the write-failure dialog — telling the author "No new session was committed. Your previous saved session is still intact" about a session that had just been committed, and offering an older one that would not come back. A `catch` spanning two operations attributes every failure to whichever one the reader thinks of first; the cost here is not a wrong message but a wrong decision taken on it. **And the honest split exposed a third thing, which is the one worth keeping:** the queue’s closes are carried out by the window going away and by nothing else — `stagedDecisions` is read at exactly one place, to shape the payload — so a window that stays open after a committed write leaves the durable session describing tabs that are still on screen, and the next ordinary background write resurrects a discarded untitled document with nobody asked. My first proposed repair was to reset the in-memory baseline to the live session, and it was wrong in a way worth recording: the baseline is not a preference, it is an assertion about what is on disk, so falsifying it would have sent the next quit down the no-write proof path with a payload the backend refuses by design — a spurious write-failure dialog, strictly worse than the bug. The right move was to change the fact rather than the record of it: re-write the live session through the same `enqueueSessionSave` lane, which updates the baseline from its own receipt. **Generalisable: when a cached value and reality disagree, ask which one is the claim and which one is the world. Editing the claim is only ever right when the claim is wrong.** **Review then found the compensating write in the wrong place twice over, and the pattern is worth naming:** it fired on the no-delta proof path, where nothing had been written and so nothing needed putting back (J2 "it does not write again"), and it carried no generation fence, so a superseded attempt could write behind a newer one. Both are the same mistake — a compensation written as an unconditional consequence of the *failure* rather than of the *thing it compensates for*. The condition on a compensating action is never "did the thing fail", it is "did the thing it undoes actually happen, and am I still the one who owns it". **And the reporting fix had the identical defect one layer out:** the two honest new messages reached the author through the app shell's `documentErrorMessage`, which prefixes "Cowrite couldn’t complete the document action" — so a refused window close was announced as a document action, naming a subject the author never acted on. Fixing the message at the reporter cannot fix a consumer that renames it; the repair is a `UserFacingError` whose message is passed through verbatim, and the function moved to `backend.ts` beside `formatActionFailure` so the branch could be pinned at its own seam rather than through three layers of App mocks. Two failed attempts at that pin are the smaller lesson: reaching a consumer's branch through whatever producer happens to be mockable buys a cell that fails for reasons unrelated to the claim.

- [2026-09-05] **A negative mock assertion taken across an `await` is asserting over a window, and anything the app legitimately queues can walk into it (#2335).** `writes nothing when a refused close follows the no-write proof` — the cell #2328's round-1 review bought to pin J2's *"it does not write again"* — failed once in a clean-worktree baseline at `bdda27ca1` and passed the next four full runs. A probe on both sides of its `saveTabSessionMock.mockClear()`, run under eight spinner processes, caught it: `PROBE_CALLS_AT_CLEAR 0`, and the one call the assertion then counted was `{tabs:[a.md], activePath:"a.md"}` — the **mount** payload. The production path had written nothing, exactly as J2 requires; the cell counted the ordinary session write the tab-persistence effect fires on mount, which `enqueueSessionSave` puts on `sessionSaveRef`'s promise chain. `await screen.findByRole("tab", …)` resolves when the tab *renders*, and a render orders nothing about a write queued behind a promise, so which of the two won was decided by machine load. **The generalisable shape is not "tests are flaky under load" — it is that `not.toHaveBeenCalled()` cannot distinguish *the code under test did not do this* from *something unrelated has not finished yet*.** A negative assertion is only as good as the window it covers, and every `await` between the clear and the assertion widens that window to include work nobody is thinking about. The fix needed no invention: this same file already had the idiom 542 lines up, at `:2270` — wait for the expected write, `await act(async () => {})`, *then* clear — so the repair was to apply the file's own pattern to the one cell that skipped it, and re-prove non-vacuity by deleting the `delta &&` gate and watching the cell go red. **The shape outlives this cell:** scanning every test file for a `mockClear()` followed across an `await` by a same-mock `not.toHaveBeenCalled()` finds four further sites, one of which is the precedent above and three of which are unexamined — and widening the shape to a negative assertion on a *different* mock than the one cleared finds more, which is worth knowing mostly because it shows the count is definition-sensitive rather than a fact. None of them has produced a failure, so they are an investigation item (#2338) and not a defect list; hardening a window nobody has seen anything walk into is how a fix for one cell becomes churn across ten. **#2338 then probed all three and found every one closed — by three different mechanisms, which is the part worth keeping.** The first is the rule the original entry was missing: **await the *call*, not a symptom of it.** Two safe sites do that — `waitFor(expect(openDocumentMock).toHaveBeenCalledWith(...))`, and a rendered string produced from the awaited call's own result — so the call is already counted before the clear, probed at 1 on every run under load with nothing further after a settle. The cell that broke waited on `findByRole("tab")`, a **render**, which is downstream of state but orders nothing about a write queued behind a promise. **The second mechanism is emptiness:** `pickSaveAsPath` in the same cell waits on nothing of its own and probes **0, not 1** — it is safe because nothing calls it anywhere inside the window, which no amount of awaiting the right thing would have achieved and no scan can infer from shape. **The third is argument narrowing:** `TabbedEditor.test.tsx:3018` clears after a render wait — structurally the *same* shape as the cell that broke — yet is closed because its assertion is `not.toHaveBeenCalledWith(..., "require_untitled_lossless")`, a third argument only the close path passes, so an ordinary session write cannot satisfy it. **So the rule is a way to close a window, not a census of how windows get closed**, and a scanner that finds a site failing it has found a question rather than a defect. Checking which mechanism applies is sometimes a one-line read (`:2953`'s `waitFor` says so on its face) and sometimes not: `App.test.tsx:662`'s render wait only orders the call once you have read `App.tsx:464-470` and seen that the rendered text comes from `discoverGrounding`'s own resolution — a two-file read, and exactly the sort of reasoning the rule is supposed to spare you. **Two smaller things worth keeping.** The failing baseline was the run that happened to overlap a `cargo build`; without that accident the flake would have stayed invisible for however long it takes to lose a review round to it — deliberately loading the machine is a cheap way to ask a suite whether its waits are real. And the reproduction cost less than the argument would have: 12 loaded runs of one cell produced the failure and the probe explained it, where reasoning about promise ordering from the source would have produced a plausible story and no evidence.

## Failed Approaches

- [2026-08-20] **#1387 round 3: could not reproduce the appended-timeline-card-absent flake a third time — closing per the issue's own stated criterion, not a root cause.** #1387 records an unexplained ~8s DOM absence of an appended `chat-message` reply card seen 4× in one #1118 session (round 1, 2026-08-1x) and again 2× in a second session differing only by doc comments (round 2, 2026-08-12); both rounds ruled out the mechanisms they could name (windowing math, the same-`setCards`-call fold, a stale dev bundle) without landing on a cause, and the issue body itself sanctions closing unreproduced: *"If it never recurs, closing as unreproducible is a fine outcome — the value here is the record, not the fix."* This round, on `origin/main` (merge-base with this branch is the tip that includes #1408): `npx playwright test -g "a chat question's answer lands in the timeline as an agent message below the run that produced it" --workers=1`, repeated 8×, deliberately run alongside concurrent CPU load (a parallel `npx vitest run src/timeline` and later a `cargo test --workspace`) to approximate round 1's "ambient machine load" condition — **7/8 passed**; the 8th failed at `Error: Timed out waiting 120000ms from config.webServer`, i.e. the test-driver crate never finished compiling/booting inside the timeout while the concurrent `cargo test --workspace` was mid-build on the same box, not the reported symptom (no page ever loaded for that attempt, so `[data-card-kind="chat-message"]` was never even queried). `npx vitest run src/timeline` (368/368) also passed clean. **One candidate mechanism from round 1's comment is now structurally retired, independent of this round's evidence:** `git log -p -- playwright.config.mjs` shows #1408 (merged 2026-08-12, the same day as round 2) changed both `webServer` entries' `reuseExistingServer` from `!process.env.CI` to a hardcoded `false` and gave every run its own allocated port pair (`scripts/e2e-ports.mjs`) — done for parallel-run port collisions, not for #1387, but it removes "a reused dev server's module graph disagreeing with a `git rebase`-moved working tree" as a live possibility for any future recurrence (round 2 had already shown empirically, via `lsof -ti tcp:1420` being empty before every one of its runs, that no server was being reused in its own occurrence either — so this closes a mechanism neither round's evidence actually depended on, rather than explaining either one). **Not attempted:** round 2's "comments-only diff" trigger (editing unrelated TS files with doc-comment-only changes) — round 2's own data already showed that trigger inconsistent (its run 3, the first run after an edit, passed), so chasing it further did not fit this card's time-box. **Net: still no reproduction, still no root cause, exactly the outcome the issue named as an acceptable close.** Recommend #1387 close on this round's record per its own stated criterion; if it recurs, the next occurrence now has three rounds of ruled-out mechanisms to build on instead of starting cold.

- [2026-07-20] **Tried to verify the nested-`spellcheck="false"` claim on a real webview (P2b-9a, #361) — this dev machine has no spellchecker, so the experiment cannot be run here at all.** The spellcheck suppression layer rests on one unexecutable assumption: that a webview resolves the `spellcheck` attribute *per node*, honouring `false` on a span nested inside a `spellcheck="true"` editing host. Review flagged it as asserted-as-fact and asked for the cheap closer — run the app, put a misspelling in frontmatter and in a `{++…++}` delimiter, look. **Every step of that was attempted and the environment defeated it.** (1) Full-screen capture via `CopyFromScreen` returns **solid black** — the console session reports `Active` but has no live framebuffer. (2) `PrintWindow` on the Edge HWND captures the title bar but a **blank white content area** — Chromium composites through the GPU into a surface `PrintWindow` can't see; fixed by relaunching with `--disable-gpu --disable-gpu-compositing`, after which the page rendered correctly in-capture. **That flag pair is the reusable trick for screenshotting a Chromium/WebView2 window on a headless-ish Windows box.** (3) With rendering finally working, **no squiggles appeared anywhere — including on the deliberately-misspelled control line with no nested spans at all.** (4) Injected input (`SetForegroundWindow` + `mouse_event` + `SendKeys`) never landed — the control's text was unchanged in the capture — so the checker was never nudged by real user input either. **Root cause, and the thing worth remembering:** `New-Object -ComObject Microsoft.Windows.SpellCheckerFactory` fails with `REGDB_E_CLASSNOTREG`, and no Hunspell `.bdic` exists or downloads under a fresh profile. There is no spellchecker on this machine, so *nothing* could squiggle. **The methodological point — the reason this is filed as a failure and not a result: the negative control saved me from shipping a false positive.** Both suppressed regions came back clean, which is exactly what "suppression works" looks like; had I only screenshotted the test case I would have reported the claim confirmed. It was clean because the checker never ran. **A pixel-absence experiment is worthless without a positive control that proves the mechanism was live** — and when the control is dead, the correct output is "not executable here", not a verdict. Resolution: the assertion was downgraded in `editor/spellcheck.ts` and the `NI-spellcheck-suppression` matrix row from stated fact to explicitly-labelled inference (HTML Living Standard: nearest explicit ancestor wins; Blink walks up from the position), carrying the "still owed on WebView2 + WKWebView" note. **Don't re-run this probe on this machine — install a spellchecker first or use a real desktop.**
- [2026-07-10] **Wrote tests + implementation together instead of failing-test-first (P1-3, #39) — TDD process violation.** For `textops::apply` I authored the full test suite and the implementation in a single pass, then proved the tests had teeth by mutating the impl (swap deletion semantics / break `decide` → the right tests fail). The *artifact* was fine, but it skipped the red→green discipline (CLAUDE.md TDD from `textops/` onward) and — worse — I noticed the tension mid-task and reasoned my way past it instead of asking, which is the **Rule #1** violation (never bend a rule without CJ's explicit permission). Mutation-testing after the fact is a *mitigation, not compliance*: TDD is a process rule and "it worked out" is not permission. **Correction:** when a rule feels worth bending, stop and ask first — even for a small, high-confidence exception; the ten-second round-trip *is* the point. (Related, same session: named downstream units — "P1-7 editor" — in the TD-11 corollary without opening the plan to confirm P1-7 was the accept/reject unit; it wasn't, P2a-5 is. Verify a referenced unit/file/flag exists before recommending it.)
- [2025-02-12] Auth polling with `setInterval(5000ms)` — GitHub returns `slow_down` which increases required interval to 35s+. Fixed by using `setTimeout` loop that fetches interval from backend before each poll.

## Deferred Fixes

- [2026-09-02, **#1746 (P2a-11b), resolved by #2231**] **Four `app_local_data_dir` stores claim one "blake3(canonical root)" key; two of them hash different bytes.** Relocating the ownership registry to `<app_local_data_dir>/instances/<project_key>/` made `store::lock` the third consumer of `store::transcript::project_key` (`blake3(canonical.to_string_lossy().as_bytes())`), alongside `store::transcript` and `store::adoption`. The fourth per-project store under the same directory — `commands::tabs`'s writer lease at `tabs.rs:187` — hand-rolls `blake3(canonical_root.as_os_str().as_encoded_bytes())` instead. The two agree for every valid-Unicode path and diverge on an unpaired surrogate (WTF-8 vs `U+FFFD`), so it is latent rather than an observed bug, but it means "the identical keying `store::transcript` already uses" (TD-20 ruling (3)) is true by convention rather than by construction. `commands::tabs` also carries a third, fallible spelling of the canonicalization rule itself. **Not fixed here:** changing the lease key renames every on-disk lease path, which needs a deliberate call about orphaning an in-flight lease — out of scope for a relocation card. What #1746 *could* dedup without that call, it did: `agent::project::canonical_root` now delegates to `store::transcript::canonical_root` rather than holding a byte-identical copy. Filed and boarded as #2231; note P2a-11c (#1747) needs a **fallible** variant of the same rule (TD-20 ruling (4): a root that cannot be canonicalized must fail before the registry is touched), so a shared home has to expose both, not collapse them. **Resolved by #2231:** `store::project_identity` is the one home — `canonical_root`, the fallible `require_canonical_root` P2a-11c needs, `project_key`, and `project_dir` — and all five consumers call it. The deferred "deliberate call about orphaning an in-flight lease" turned out not to be needed: the unified key hashes the canonical root's UTF-8 bytes, which both old spellings already produced for every valid-Unicode root, so no lease path and no transcript directory moves.

- [2026-09-02, **#1746 (P2a-11b), closed at PR #2233 review round 1**] **`OpenProject`'s ownership-release ordering is bought by struct field order — and "no test pins the order" was a real vacuity, not a theoretical one.** TD-20 ruling (2) requires that releasing ownership never precede unbinding the MCP server; the mechanism is Rust's field-declaration drop order, with `_ownership` declared last. I recorded this as an unguarded axis and the Reviewer *proved* it: moving `_ownership` from last to first left the whole lib target at **671 passed, 0 failed**, so the named falsifier ("an ownership release that leaves the MCP server bound") was falsified by nothing. The lesson is about which fix to reach for. The expensive one — `server: Option<ProjectMcpServer>` plus a hand-written `Drop` to sequence teardown explicitly — was correctly declined; the cheap one is a **source-text assertion over the declaration order**, in the same style as this PR's own `store::gitignore::caller_set_tests`, and it costs one test. **Generalizable:** when a property is bought by a language rule rather than by code (drop order, field order, trait coherence, module privacy), the behavioural tests will pass at every reshuffle — pin the *declaration* by reading it, and verify the pin reddens under the reshuffle rather than trusting its shape.

- [2026-09-02, **#1746 (P2a-11b), PR #2233 review round 2**] **A guard's own parser is an axis, and a parser that discards what it cannot read fails open.** Round 1's fix for the vacuous drop-order falsifier read `OpenProject`'s field names out of the source, then filtered out any parsed name containing whitespace — a tidy-looking way to skip blank lines and stray punctuation. It also silently discarded every *qualified* declaration: `pub probe_marker: Arc<u32>` parsed to the name `pub probe_marker`, was dropped, and left `fields.last()` reading `_ownership`. The Reviewer appended exactly that field after `_ownership` — the precise hazard the test exists to catch — and the cell returned `ok. 1 passed`. The non-vacuity mutation I *had* run (moving the field to first) passes through the same parser unqualified, so it proved the assertion could fail without ever exercising the parser's blind spot. **Generalizable, and the sharper form of the round-1 lesson:** when a guard reads text rather than asking the compiler, the reading is part of the guard, and it must *fail closed* — treat "I could not parse this" as a failure, never as "there was nothing there." A sentinel over inputs that parse cleanly (here `_doc_watcher`/`server`/`watchdog`) confirms the extraction ran; it says nothing about what the extraction threw away. And when choosing the mutation that proves such a guard, pick one whose *input shape* differs, not only one whose value differs.

- [2026-09-02, **#1746 (P2a-11b), corrected at PR #2233 review round 1**] **"A Windows directory symlink needs Developer Mode" is true of `mklink /D` and false of a junction — do not let a true fact about one mechanism excuse an untested axis.** I skipped criterion 5's symlink identity axis and wrote that justification into the PR body. It is accurate about `mklink /D` / `std::os::windows::fs::symlink_dir`, and irrelevant: `mklink /J` creates a junction with no elevation and no Developer Mode, and `canonicalize` resolves junctions exactly as it resolves symlinks. The Reviewer appended the junction test at my head and it passed first time — the behaviour was already correct, only the pin was missing, so the cost of the wrong reason was a whole review round for a test that could have shipped. **Generalizable:** a stated reason for not testing an axis is itself a claim, and gets the same standard of proof as an assertion. "Privileged operation" in particular is usually about one *API*, not the capability — check for the unprivileged sibling before writing the axis off.

- [2026-08-20, **#1379 — this entry is the "what was learned" half**] **The untitled/scratch-document lifecycle (D12, FR-ED-8) shipped narrower than its own issue text implied, deliberately.** #1379's "What this card is" cites `store::service`'s pre-minted scratch doc id (`mint_doc_id`/`adopt`) as the intended adoption mechanism, and asks whether the Save As surface is a J2 delta needing Designer sign-off. Investigation found `mint_doc_id`/`adopt` already fully built and tested (P2a-10, #145) but with **zero production callers** — the same "designed, half-built, no production caller" shape #781/D26's `store::lock` was in before that card wired it up. The reason nothing calls it: an untitled document today has no comment/track-change UI at all (`RecoveredDocument` was a read-only `<pre>`), so nothing can accumulate against a scratch id *before* it has a path — which makes `mint_doc_id` mint-early-for-nothing-to-carry, functionally identical in this increment to the ordinary `SidecarStore::get_or_mint_for_path` every other first-touched document already goes through at save time. Shipped scope: `RecoveredDocument` → `UntitledDocument` (`src/editor/UntitledDocument.tsx`), a plain `<textarea>` (not `EditorShell`/CM6) whose every edit persists directly into the session snapshot (its only durable copy — no autosave target exists yet), with one `[Save As…]` action reusing the *unmodified* `saveDocument` IPC command and `stores/tabs.ts`'s new `adoptUntitledTab` to become an ordinary project tab through the exact rail/active-marker path `TabbedEditor.handleSavedAs` already gives an ordinary document's Save As. No new Rust command, no `mint_doc_id`/`adopt` production wiring, no CM6/rich-text parity for the untitled buffer — all three are real follow-up (filed and boarded, not silently dropped), because building them in this same PR would have meant retrofitting `EditorShell`'s ~1,700-line autosave/external-change/Missing-on-disk machinery to a document with no project path, which is the "new mechanism… worth checking with CJ" the issue itself flagged, and a materially different, materially larger diff than "make the existing pane editable and adoptable." That initial Save As attribution did not survive review: CJ's later 2026-08-21 ruling makes Save As the ratified product decision for this pane and explicitly says FR-ED-8 does not itself prescribe Save As.

- [2026-08-26, **#1379 semantic-integration review — one serialization lane still requires capture before its first wait**] The independent exact-head review found that the earlier integration had kept `createSaveRunner` in front of #1822's `sessionSaveRef` lane. Although only one `saveTabSession` call edge existed, that was still two queues: while untitled write A was in flight, edit B plus blur left only an empty token in the front runner; a project switch before A settled could then make the delayed callback suppress B for the next root's pending restore or materialize it from the next project's refs, losing the old untitled buffer's only durable copy. A deterministic cell holds A's untitled-triggered write in flight, performs B plus blur and switches projects, then proves B is written to the old root. The correction removes the redundant runner: the scheduler now captures `{root, payload}` immediately and submits it to #1822's existing lane, which remains the sole serializer and still owns pending-restore suppression. General lesson: a single backend call edge is not proof of a single queue, and identity-bearing state must be captured before *any* wait that can outlive a root transition.

- [2026-08-15, **carded as #1599 — this entry is the "what was learned" half**] **Investigating #1595 measured a second, independent breach of the same FR-ED-7 sentence, on a path with no Missing episode in it at all.** While the changed-contents banner is showing and the user has answered neither `[Reload]` nor `[Keep mine]`, the next autosave writes the buffer straight over the disk bytes the banner is offering: driven end-to-end (open a document, overwrite its file out of band, wait for the banner, type one character), the disk ends up holding `"Original disk bytesX"` and the concurrent bytes are gone. `triggerSave` consults `missingRef` — and now #1595's write pause — but never `externalChange`. Verified against `66b77818e` and again against #1595's branch head, since that repair addresses a different trigger and deliberately does not change this one. **Not folded into #1595**, whose acceptance explicitly bounds its pause at "until FR-ED-7 divergence handling is installed": what FR-ED-7 then *does* with the disk side is a wider behavior change (every autosave while any divergence is undecided) with a real UX consequence to settle against `docs/ux-design.md` — edits made under the banner would not persist until the user answers — rather than a line to slip into a race fix. `handleKeepMine`'s existing comment records that after an explicit "Keep mine" the next save is *meant* to write past the disk state, so the open question is only the undecided window. **The reusable half is how it was found:** the probe that isolated #1595's ordering was run a second time with the Missing episode removed, purely to check whether the pause was the only thing standing between an autosave and a foreign file. It was not — and one extra run of an existing probe was the whole cost of learning that.

- [2026-08-07, **consciously accepted, no card filed — recorded so a later reader knows they were seen**] **PR #1237 (#1234) left five review Minors unaddressed, all below the bar for another head move.** (1) The set-equality guard's bespoke `unexpected`/`missing` hint degenerates to `unexpected: [], missing: []` when an entry is **duplicated** — `filter`/`includes` cannot express a duplicate — though `deepStrictEqual`'s own array diff still prints, so the failure is never blind. (2) The block-capture regex requires `paths:` to be the first key under `pull_request:`, so inserting a legitimate `branches:` above it fails at `assert.ok` rather than at the set comparison — fail-closed, but brittle. (3) The same regex uses a literal `\n`, so a CRLF checkout would fail `assert.ok`; unreachable today because `.gitattributes` line 2 is `* text=auto eol=lf` and a pre-existing assertion in the same file already carries the identical dependency and passes on the Windows runner. (4) The guard pins the `pull_request` path set only; a separate `push:` trigger carrying `docs/test-matrix.md` passes. (5) A doc-placement nit: the inserted rationale splits an existing operational prose run. Each was re-checked at the final head and none had grown into an Important. The card cost three heads (`80ab2340` Sound → `27622369` Needs Minor Revision → `fbab9b47` Sound) for a four-file change; spending a fourth on cosmetics was not worth the SHA-bound re-review. **Separately NOT fixed here and correctly so:** `docs/ci-windows-runner.md:288-290` claims *"the canary's bounded artifact wait lets the peer start when that job releases it."* Run `31185275886` disproves it — **but not in the way the sentence's own framing suggests, and getting this wrong is the trap.** The production job freed `cowrite-windows-2` at 14:53:33 and the peer job **did** start at 14:53:35, a full **18 seconds before** base's 14:53:53 deadline; it then published its readiness artifact at 14:54:02, **9 seconds too late**. So the peer was released in time and still lost. The wait must cover not merely the peer's queue time but its **checkout-and-publish latency after starting** (~27s here) — which is exactly why #1231 rules out raising the `90`-attempt budget as the lever and asks for a neutral exit instead. A documented assumption falsified by #1231, and #1231's to fix, not a trigger-scoping card's.

- [2026-07-21] **`rangeOf(content, substr)` is copy-pasted into six test files (`App.test.tsx`, `trackedChanges.test.ts`, `spellcheck.test.ts`, `structuralDecorations.test.ts`, `RedlineReview.test.tsx`, `TrackChangesBulkMenu.test.tsx`) — well past CLAUDE.md's Rule of Three, and one file's copy documents itself as "mirrors `editor/trackedChanges.test.ts`'s `rangeOf`", so the duplication is known and deliberate rather than accidental.** Noticed during #447's simplify pass, where a seventh copy would have been the natural thing to write (avoided — that card's stand-in parser derives its offsets from hoisted marker constants instead). Not extracted there: it would mean editing six test files that the card's own diff never touched, on a bugfix whose scope was three call sites. The extraction is mechanical (a shared `src/editor/testRanges.ts` or similar, alongside `src/test-setup.ts`), and three of the copies already carry a compatible `occurrence` parameter, so they'd collapse into one signature cleanly. Worth its own small card.
  **Resolved by #563:** extracted to `src/editor/testRanges.ts` (its own `occurrence`-param signature, defaulting to 0, absorbs all six call sites without special-casing) and imported by all six; no genuinely divergent semantics turned up. Two of the six (`RedlineReview.test.tsx`, `TrackChangesBulkMenu.test.tsx`) previously returned a silent `{from: -1, …}` instead of throwing on a not-found substring — dead code in both, since every call site's substring is always present — so unifying on the throwing form changes no observed behavior. Added `src/editor/testRanges.test.ts` to pin the shared helper's own behavior directly. `npx vitest run` reports the same 1003 pre-existing tests unchanged, plus 4 new ones for the helper itself.
- [2026-07-18] **P2a-5 popover accept/reject drops DOM focus, breaking the next Cmd/Ctrl+S (found while e2e-verifying #157/P2a-8).** **Resolved by #239:** `RedlineReview.tsx`'s `decide()` now calls `view.focus()` after `decideChange` whenever the decision came from an open popover (toolbar-driven decisions never moved focus, so they're untouched); see `NI-redline-popover-focus` in `docs/test-matrix.md`. After accepting or rejecting a change from the `RedlinePopover` (not the toolbar — the toolbar buttons don't blur the editor), the popover unmounts and nothing refocuses the CM6 `contentDOM`; `document.activeElement` ends up on `<body>`. Since the `Mod-s` binding is a CM6 `keymap` extension (only reachable while the editor's own DOM has focus), a manual save right after a popover decision silently does nothing — reproduced via a standalone Playwright script (`document.activeElement.className === ""` post-accept) and via the existing e2e suite (`e2e/acceptance.spec.mjs`'s "renders CriticMarkup as redline..." test hangs at the post-accept `Cmd+s` disk-content assertion). Confirmed present on `main` (pre-#157) too — not a P2a-8 regression. Root cause + fix (refocus `view.dom` or `view.focus()` when a popover-driven decision closes it) belongs to P2a-5's owner/a follow-up card, not bundled into #157 (different concern: popover focus management, not source-view/display-mode). Filed here rather than fixed in place per CLAUDE.md scope discipline (smallest reasonable change; one thing per PR). *Also fixed in the same #157 pass, since it directly blocked verifying #157's own e2e addition and was a one-line, clearly-scoped `EditorShell.tsx` gap:* the redline toolbar's pending-change count never refreshed after the *initial* async `parse_changes` seed landed (a pure `seedTrackedChanges`/`dropTrackedChanges` dispatch moves neither `doc` nor selection, so `EditorShell`'s `updateListener` bump — which only watched `docChanged`/`selectionSet` — never re-rendered the toolbar; a document opened with pending changes on disk silently showed "0 / 0" until an unrelated edit or caret move happened to nudge a re-render). Regression test: `EditorShell.test.tsx`'s "async redline seed" suite.
- [2026-07-10] **TD-11 implementation follow-ups (ratified 2026-07-10, CJ).** The P1-3 (#39) review surfaced that CriticMarkup can't safely embed arbitrary text (no escape; first-closer-wins nesting). Decision recorded as **TD-11** (reject-at-delivery, never escape/allow). Two consumers must honor it: **(a) P2a-13** `propose_changes` — add the delivery-time **whole-set** round-trip validation (pure `textops` predicate: the rendered change set parses back to exactly the intended changes + the accepted projection is delimiter-clean; evaluated over the whole set, since two individually-clean adjacent insertions can jointly form a marker). Refuse-unrepresentable splits by where the delimiter lives: **payload-caused** (insertion/`new`) → return to the agent to reword; **existing-text-caused** (delimiter in the deleted/`old` range — the agent can't fix it, and a bare `--}` has no single-marker form) → honest hard-fail for that edit, never corrupt. Named test under P6. **(b) P2a-5 / editor accept-reject wiring** (redline + accept/reject popover + keyboard triage) — apply *interactive/queued* decisions against a single parse snapshot or a stable change identity, never re-parse-then-positional-index (else a revealed marker misattributes a later decision). Read-only display-mode projections (P1-7) and the P1-11 delivery partition-apply are batch/whole-document, so they're safe by construction. The `textops::apply` module doc carries the caveat; the batch `apply` is correct today, so nothing is broken now — these are constraints on the *future* editor and delivery code. **Correction:** first drafts of this (and the `textops::apply` module doc on #79) named "P1-7" for the editor consumer — P1-7 is display-mode transforms (read-only); the interactive accept/reject wiring is P2a-5. The #43 heads-up comment was corrected accordingly.
- [2025-02-12] Several Rust dead code warnings (get_username, add_remote, open, workdir, token_type/scope fields). These are utility functions for future use — not urgent.
- [2026-07-17] **Copilot CLI auth-state detection is a conservative env-token approximation, not a verified read-only status probe (P2a-22, #158) — recommend a small follow-up spike.** `agent::providers::copilot_auth_from_env` covers the CLI's documented headless precedence (`COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN`/`GH_TOKEN`/`GITHUB_TOKEN`) but under-reads a real interactive `copilot login` (credential-store-backed) session — see the matching Technical Insights entry for the live-verification note. A follow-up spike should determine a genuinely cheap, side-effect-free way to read that stored session (a future Copilot CLI release may add a `status` subcommand the way Claude Code's `auth status` already exists; failing that, investigate the credential-store location/format directly) before this chip is claimed fully accurate for FR-X-9 sign-off.
- [2026-07-18] **P2a-5 redline toolbar count can permanently show `0 / 0` after opening a document with pending CriticMarkup — a pre-existing bug, confirmed on `main` tip (a0cfbf3), unrelated to #158.** Found while re-verifying `e2e/acceptance.spec.mjs` after a #158 rebase-loopback (round 1): `EditorShell.tsx` seeds `trackedChangesField` asynchronously (`parseChanges(...).then(... dispatch({ effects: seedTrackedChanges.of(...) }))`), but its `EditorView.updateListener` only bumps the React `redlineVersion` counter that drives `RedlineReview`'s re-render on `update.docChanged || update.selectionSet` — an effects-only seed dispatch trips neither, so React never re-renders to pick up the freshly-seeded change set. CodeMirror's own decorations *do* render correctly regardless (`.cm-redlineInsert` shows the right text) because CM6 redraws from the `StateField` on every transaction independent of React; only the React-side toolbar count is stuck at the pre-seed `0 / 0` until some unrelated doc-change/selection event happens to fire later. `RedlineReview.test.tsx`'s `mount()` helper dispatches the seed *before* the first render, so this unit test never exercises the real race and passes regardless. Reproduced deterministically via a standalone Playwright debug script against both this branch and a fresh `origin/main` checkout with warm dev/test-driver servers — confirmed identical on both, so this is not a #158 regression. Filed as #237 for a separate engineer to pick up (likely fix: also bump `redlineVersion` when a transaction carries a `seedTrackedChanges`/`dropTrackedChanges` effect, not just on `docChanged`/`selectionSet`). Not fixed here — out of scope for #158's rebase-loopback and touches P2a-5's already-reviewed-Sound code, not this card's diff.

- [2026-07-18] **`textops::deliver::properties::prop_delivered_subset_is_never_corrupt` is a pre-existing, seed-dependent proptest flake — found while baseline-checking for #163/P2a-19, unrelated to `store::comments`.** A `cargo test --workspace` baseline run hit `Test failed: kept marker 4..11 truncated away at crates\textops\src\deliver.rs:727` for `live = "     0"` with two `EditSpec`s (one replacing with `"~~}"`). Confirmed pre-existing and unrelated to this card's diff: `git stash`-ing every P2a-19 change (all confined to `store::comments`/`commands::comments`) and re-running just this test against the untouched tree still surfaces it (the failure is a genuine, rare edge case in `textops::deliver`'s delimiter-injection handling — a payload replacement introducing `~~}` can apparently truncate an unrelated already-kept marker — not something P2a-19 touches or introduced). Proptest doesn't pin a fixed seed here, so the case doesn't reproduce every run; deleted the regression file this run generated (`crates/textops/proptest-regressions/deliver.txt`, untracked) rather than committing an unrelated pin as part of this card. Needs a `textops::deliver` owner to reproduce deterministically (fix proptest's seed or hardcode the minimal failing case as a unit test) and fix the underlying truncation — likely related to TD-11's "delimiter can appear mid-payload" concern already tracked in the 2026-07-10 TD-11 entry above, but confirm before assuming.

- [2026-07-18] **#163/P2a-19's `[Address with AI]` only lands in the timeline comment card, not "the editor's comment popover" the issue's acceptance text also names — there is no such popover to wire into yet.** `src/editor/` has zero comment-related code today: P2a-18 (#162) already scoped itself to "wires the UI trigger and result rendering only," explicitly deferring live in-editor selection/anchoring UI as a P2a-1 follow-up (see that card's FR-CMT-1 test-matrix note), so comment threads render solely through `timeline/CommentCard`/`comments/CommentThreadCard` — there is no editor-anchored comment surface (`RedlinePopover` is the *tracked-change* accept/reject popover, a different concern) for an "Address with AI" trigger to live in. Building that popover from scratch is a distinct, larger editor feature (selection-anchored UI, CM6 positioning, a new component) outside a single ≤4h card and outside #163's own dependency list (P2a-18, P2a-14 only) — implementing it here would be scope creep past what a focused review could verify. `[Address with AI]` is fully wired everywhere a comment thread currently renders (every open/orphaned card, regardless of provenance — own/finding/reviewer — per FR-CMT-2), which is the acceptance criterion's substance; the "in the editor's comment popover" surface itself is a follow-up once P2a-1's live-selection/in-editor-comment-anchoring work lands. Flagging for a follow-up issue rather than silently dropping it.
